# Prayut Chan-o-cha — Thailand, 2023

## 1. Overview

General Prayut Chan-o-cha remained Thailand’s prime minister at the start of 2023 and governed through a military-backed coalition under the 2017 constitution. He was also leader of the United Thai Nation Party, while rival General Prawit Wongsuwan led Palang Pracharath. Prayut’s influence was substantial but constrained by the monarchy, the military establishment, a junta-appointed Senate, coalition bargaining, the Constitutional Court, and powerful bureaucratic and security institutions.

The May 2023 election was competitive and produced a decisive rejection of the military-backed parties: Move Forward won the most House seats, 151, while Prayut’s party won 36. The military-appointed Senate then helped prevent Move Forward leader Pita Limjaroenrat from forming a government. Pheu Thai subsequently formed a coalition that included former military-aligned parties, and Srettha Thavisin became prime minister in August. Prayut therefore governed most of the requested period but left office after the election and transition.

The strongest overall interpretation is continuity rather than policy transformation: economic recovery continued, especially through tourism, but political liberalization remained sharply limited and the government did not resolve the southern insurgency, corruption concerns, or the institutional dominance of the military and monarchy.

## 2. Evidence records

### Record PRAYUT-2023-01 — Prayut’s authority was institutionally constrained by the military-monarchical order

**Fact.** Prayut formally headed the cabinet and controlled the executive branch, but his effective authority operated within a political system in which the monarchy, military, appointed Senate, Constitutional Court, and senior bureaucracy retained decisive influence. The 2017 constitution allowed the junta-appointed Senate to participate in choosing the prime minister. Prayut was influential within the military but depended on coalition partners and institutional allies.

**Why it matters.** Government outcomes cannot be attributed to Prayut alone. His authority was real, but embedded in a broader military-monarchical system.

**Strongest source.** *BTI 2024 Country Report: Thailand*, Bertelsmann Stiftung, 2024, pp. 3–10. The report describes the junta-created constitution, appointed Senate, military influence, coalition structure, and Prayut’s continuing political role. It covers February 2021–January 2023, so it is primarily inherited-period evidence rather than a full 2023 assessment: [BTI 2024 Thailand report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

**Connection to ruler.** Strong for the institutional conditions under which Prayut governed; weaker for decisions made after January 2023.

**Complicating evidence.** Prayut still exercised ordinary executive powers, led the cabinet, represented Thailand internationally, and remained the candidate of a political party. The military-backed structure did not make him merely symbolic.

**Caution.** BTI is an expert comparative assessment and contains interpretive judgments. Its reporting period ends before most of 2023.

**Independent support.** Freedom House describes the 2023 elections as comparatively open but states that the military-appointed Senate blocked the leading opposition party from forming a government: [Freedom House Thailand country profile](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand).

### Record PRAYUT-2023-02 — The 2023 election rejected the military-backed parties, but the result did not immediately determine government formation

**Fact.** The May 2023 general election was competitive and produced a major opposition victory. Move Forward won 151 of the 500 House seats, ahead of Pheu Thai. Prayut’s United Thai Nation Party won 36 seats. Move Forward’s attempt to form a government was blocked when Pita failed to secure sufficient support from the combined House and military-appointed Senate. Pheu Thai later formed a coalition including parties associated with the former military-backed government, and Srettha Thavisin became prime minister in August.

**Why it matters.** The election demonstrated substantial popular support for political change while also showing that the constitutional system limited the electoral transfer of power.

**Strongest source.** Associated Press reporting on the election result records Move Forward’s 151 seats and the party’s anti-military reform platform: [AP election report](https://time.com/6279758/thailand-elections-move-forward-party-pheu-thai/). Freedom House provides the institutional explanation for why the leading party was unable to form a government: [Freedom House Thailand](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand).

**Connection to ruler.** Strong. The election assessed Prayut’s government and ended his premiership, although the Senate and coalition negotiations—not Prayut acting alone—determined the successor.

**Complicating evidence.** The election itself was more open and competitive than the 2019 election, and the eventual transfer to a new civilian cabinet occurred through constitutional procedures rather than a coup.

**Caution.** AP and Freedom House summarize a rapidly changing political process; official election records and parliamentary voting data should be retained for final seat and vote totals.

**Independent support.** The UK House of Commons Library briefing and ANFREL observation reports were identified in the candidate catalogue and should be used to verify electoral administration and the prime-ministerial vote.

### Record PRAYUT-2023-03 — Repression and prosecution of political dissent continued

**Fact.** Thai authorities continued prosecuting protesters, activists, and social-media users under lèse-majesté, sedition, computer-crime, and public-assembly laws. Human Rights Watch reports that at least 258 people were prosecuted in 2023 in connection with democracy protests or online comments under lèse-majesté charges.

**Why it matters.** The government preserved a restrictive political environment despite the election and the formal end of the COVID-era emergency decree.

**Strongest source.** *World Report 2024: Thailand*, Human Rights Watch, published 2024, section “Thailand.” It is a retrospective account of 2023: [Human Rights Watch Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate to strong. Prayut’s government maintained the legal and policing framework, but prosecution decisions involved police, prosecutors, courts, and the broader security establishment.

**Complicating evidence.** The 2023 election allowed meaningful opposition campaigning and produced an opposition plurality, indicating that political competition was not completely eliminated.

**Caution.** Prosecution totals may change as cases are reclassified or updated. Court records should be consulted for individual cases and outcomes.

**Independent support.** BTI reports extensive use of lèse-majesté, sedition, emergency, and computer-crime laws against protesters and describes resulting self-censorship: [BTI report, pp. 8–10](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

### Record PRAYUT-2023-04 — The southern insurgency remained unresolved, with limited diplomatic progress

**Fact.** Violence continued in Thailand’s Malay-Muslim southern provinces. During Prayut’s premiership, the government continued peace discussions facilitated by Malaysia; a February 2023 meeting brought Thai representatives and the BRN insurgent movement together. The conflict remained active and unresolved, despite some reduction in violence during parts of 2023.

**Why it matters.** The state retained territorial control but failed to achieve a durable settlement or eliminate a long-running domestic security risk.

**Strongest source.** Associated Press reporting on Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim’s February 2023 visit to Bangkok describes the insurgency, its death toll, the peace effort, and allegations of abuses by security forces: [AP report on Thailand’s southern violence](https://apnews.com/article/ec28a896b55e42e564aa6c1d7ceff760).

**Connection to ruler.** Strong for the government’s diplomatic and security policy, though responsibility was distributed among the military, National Security Council, police, local officials, Malaysia, and insurgent organizations.

**Complicating evidence.** The government did maintain negotiations rather than relying exclusively on military operations. Later reporting indicates that violence declined in parts of the first half of 2023.

**Caution.** Conflict death counts vary by source and definition. Official security reporting may undercount civilian harm, while activist monitoring may have incomplete access.

**Independent support.** The official Southern Border Provinces Peace Dialogue Panel records the February 2023 meeting: [Thai government peace-dialogue record](https://www.southpeace.go.th/?p=70308). BTI provides longer-run context on the conflict and state limitations: [BTI report, pp. 5–7](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

### Record PRAYUT-2023-05 — Economic recovery was real but incomplete and structurally constrained

**Fact.** Thailand’s economy recovered in 2023 through domestic demand and returning tourism. The World Bank projected growth of 3.9% for 2023, following 2.6% growth in 2022. It also identified high household debt, aging, climate risks, and weak export competitiveness as continuing structural problems. Public debt reached approximately 61.2% of GDP in the first half of 2023.

**Why it matters.** The government presided over post-pandemic recovery, but the improvement did not demonstrate resolution of longer-term stagnation or inequality.

**Strongest source.** *Thailand Economic Monitor: Coping with Floods and Droughts*, World Bank, June 2023, including the growth forecast and fiscal indicators: [World Bank Economic Monitor PDF](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099062723120515431/pdf/P179738001112b0950889d0dcc60289c10e.pdf).

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. Economic performance reflects government policy but also tourism normalization, global demand, monetary conditions, and private-sector behavior.

**Complicating evidence.** The World Bank reported a strong first-quarter rebound and improving tourism, while the OECD described fiscal support as preventing recession: [World Bank June 2023 update](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/06/28/thai-economy-to-accelerate-in-2023-as-demand-from-major-economies-picks-up-tourists-return), [OECD Economic Survey 2023](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/2023/12/oecd-economic-surveys-thailand-2023_a4fadf86-en.html).

**Caution.** A 2023 forecast is not the same as a final outcome. Current-price indicators and tourism recovery should not be interpreted automatically as real improvements in household welfare.

### Record PRAYUT-2023-06 — Corruption and police accountability remained major controversies

**Fact.** Corruption allegations involving police, immigration officials, illicit Chinese business networks, and online gambling were prominent in 2023. Reports described investigations involving immigration officers accused of facilitating improper visas and allegations of police links to criminal networks. Public critics argued that Prayut had not delivered on earlier anti-corruption promises.

**Why it matters.** The controversies directly challenged the government’s claims of restoring order and integrity, while also raising questions about whether senior security institutions were effectively accountable.

**Strongest source.** Channel NewsAsia’s report on the Taiwanese actress case describes allegations of police abuse and corruption and places the issue within the broader structure of the Royal Thai Police: [CNA report](https://www.channelnewsasia.com/asia/thailand-taiwan-actress-charlene-police-corruption-3286151).

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. The prime minister had formal responsibility for the police system, but individual criminal allegations require separate proof and cannot automatically be attributed to Prayut personally.

**Complicating evidence.** Investigations and dismissals show that some state institutions did respond to allegations. Allegations in press reports are not equivalent to convictions.

**Caution.** Much of the evidence originated with whistleblowers, politically interested actors, or investigative reporting. Court and anti-corruption commission files are needed before treating specific allegations as established facts.

**Independent support.** BTI reports weak and politicized anti-corruption enforcement and limited accountability for powerful military-associated figures: [BTI report, pp. 10–11](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

### Record PRAYUT-2023-07 — Government capacity and public services were unevenly distributed

**Fact.** Thailand maintained broad access to basic administration, health, education, water, electricity, and sanitation, but service quality and opportunity remained heavily concentrated in Bangkok and a small number of urban areas. BTI describes centralized administration, bureaucratic corruption and lethargy, and unequal access to strong schools, hospitals, and jobs.

**Why it matters.** State capacity was not absent, but the government’s effectiveness was uneven and did not eliminate regional and class disparities.

**Strongest source.** BTI 2024 Thailand report, sections on basic administration and social conditions, especially pp. 6 and 24: [BTI report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. These conditions were inherited and structural, though Prayut’s cabinet was responsible for policy and budgeting during the period.

**Complicating evidence.** Thailand’s universal health coverage and administrative reach provided substantial national service provision. The National Health Security Office’s 2023 achievement report is a useful official counterpoint: [NHSO 2023 achievements](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US).

**Caution.** National access indicators can obscure differences in quality, affordability, waiting times, and regional distribution.

### Record PRAYUT-2023-08 — Information was available but politically constrained

**Fact.** Reliable information about elections, repression, corruption, and security abuses was obtainable from international organizations, courts, observers, and independent reporting, but the information environment was difficult. Criminal defamation, lèse-majesté, sedition, computer-crime laws, surveillance, and intimidation encouraged self-censorship. State and military institutions also controlled or influenced important information channels.

**Why it matters.** Reported facts should be separated from allegations, official claims, and political messaging. Underreporting is especially plausible for torture, police misconduct, corruption involving powerful actors, and violence in the Deep South.

**Strongest source.** BTI documents legal restrictions, self-censorship, politicized courts, and limited transparency; Human Rights Watch documents 2023 prosecutions: [BTI report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf), [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Connection to ruler.** Moderate. Prayut’s government maintained the legal and institutional environment, but censorship and information control were distributed across police, prosecutors, courts, regulators, military bodies, and private media owners.

**Complicating evidence.** Thailand retained active civil-society organizations, international observers, independent journalists, and competitive electoral politics.

**Caution.** International reporting may overrepresent Bangkok and high-profile political cases, while rural, migrant, minority, and southern communities receive less coverage.

## 3. Later retrospective evidence

- Human Rights Watch’s 2024 country chapter records the number of 2023 lèse-majesté prosecutions and assesses the Constitutional Court’s role in the post-election process. It should be treated as a later retrospective assessment, not contemporaneous evidence available to Prayut’s cabinet.
- BTI 2024 is a retrospective comparative report whose formal coverage ends on 31 January 2023. It is valuable for inherited institutional conditions but should not be used as a complete account of the remainder of 2023.
- Freedom House’s later country profile interprets the 2023 election as comparatively open but institutionally unable to displace the military-appointed Senate’s influence.

## 4. Important questions for deeper research

1. What were the official final vote totals, seat allocations, and parliamentary votes on the prime ministerial nominations?
2. Which decisions during the election and transition were personally directed by Prayut, and which were made by the Election Commission, Senate, courts, military, or coalition parties?
3. What were the final 2023 GDP-growth, tourism, poverty, wage, inflation, and household-debt outcomes?
4. How many protest-related prosecutions were filed, dismissed, acquitted, or still pending, and what were the defendants’ conditions of bail?
5. What civilian-casualty, military-casualty, and insurgent-casualty figures are supported by Deep South Watch, government, and independent sources?
6. Did 2023 peace talks produce measurable reductions in violence or institutional concessions?
7. What investigations into police corruption, illegal immigration facilitation, gambling, and Chinese criminal networks reached formal findings?
8. What did the State Audit Office and National Anti-Corruption Commission conclude about major procurement and infrastructure projects?
9. How did regional inequality affect health, education, and welfare outcomes during the recovery?
10. What did Prayut’s party manifesto and final policy statements promise, and which promises were implemented before his departure?

## 5. Corroborating sources opened and useful mainly for support

- [BTI 2024 Country Report: Thailand](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf) — institutional structure, repression, corruption, administration, and inherited conditions.
- [Human Rights Watch, World Report 2024: Thailand](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand) — 2023 prosecutions, election framework, southern conflict, and police-abuse concerns.
- [Freedom House, Thailand](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand) — election competitiveness and the Senate’s role.
- [World Bank, Thailand Economic Monitor, June 2023](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099062723120515431/pdf/P179738001112b0950889d0dcc60289c10e.pdf) — growth, debt, fiscal conditions, and structural constraints.
- [OECD Economic Surveys: Thailand 2023](https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/2023/12/oecd-economic-surveys-thailand-2023_a4fadf86-en.html) — fiscal support, recovery, and household debt.
- [Associated Press, Anwar Ibrahim and southern Thailand violence](https://apnews.com/article/ec28a896b55e42e564aa6c1d7ceff760) — diplomacy and insurgency.
- [Thai Southern Border Provinces Peace Dialogue record](https://www.southpeace.go.th/?p=70308) — official account of the February 2023 talks.
- [National Health Security Office, 2023 achievements](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US) — official health-service evidence.
- [CNA, police corruption and the Taiwanese actress case](https://www.channelnewsasia.com/asia/thailand-taiwan-actress-charlene-police-corruption-3286151) — police accountability controversy.

## 6. Promising leads still needing inspection

- ANFREL’s full 2023 election-observation report and interim report.
- The UK House of Commons Library briefing on the 2023 election.
- Official Election Commission results and Senate voting records.
- State Audit Office performance and procurement reports for fiscal year 2023.
- Thailand’s National Anti-Corruption Commission case records.
- Deep South Watch’s 2023 incident database.
- The Royal Thai Government policy-statement archive.
- The U.S. State Department’s 2023 Investment Climate Statement for Thailand.
- The Constitutional Court’s 2023 election-related decisions.
- Thai court records for lèse-majesté and protest cases.
- Official 2023 statistics from the National Economic and Social Development Council, National Statistical Office, and Bank of Thailand.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

# Chapter 1B — Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was prime minister of Thailand until the formation of Srettha Thavisin’s government in August 2023. He also served as minister of defence during the final phase of his government. Thailand had no nuclear weapons, no nuclear-powered military fleet, and no operating commercial nuclear-power reactor in 2023. Its relevant catastrophic-risk responsibilities therefore concerned:

- preventing nuclear proliferation;
- maintaining safeguards over civilian nuclear material and radioactive sources;
- supporting nuclear disarmament and test-ban regimes;
- controlling military and dual-use technology;
- managing cyber, biological, and other security risks; and
- avoiding procurement and foreign-policy decisions that could create strategic escalation or safety exposure.

The principal inherited posture was one of formal nonproliferation and participation in international nuclear institutions, combined with modest domestic nuclear activity and limited transparency on security-sector decisions. The most concrete 2023 evidence points to continuity and incremental strengthening of nuclear-security arrangements, not a major change in national doctrine. There is no evidence that Prayut authorized nuclear threats, nuclear coercion, proliferation, or a nuclear-weapons program.

Attribution is limited. Nuclear and cyber institutions were distributed among the Office of Atoms for Peace, the National Security Council, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the armed forces, and the cabinet. Prayut could set cabinet and defence priorities, but technical implementation and international treaty work were conducted by specialized agencies and diplomats.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Period/status | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-1B-01 | Thailand remained a non-nuclear-weapon state with safeguards and nonproliferation commitments | Inherited and target-period context | 1B.1, 1B.4, 1B.6, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-02 | Thailand publicly supported the NPT, TPNW, CTBT, and nuclear-weapon-free-zone framework in 2023 | Target period | 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4 |
| WEB-1B-03 | Thailand concluded a 2023–2029 IAEA Country Programme Framework | Target period | 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-04 | An IAEA advisory mission found a robust nuclear-security framework but identified improvements | Target period, reported December 2023 | 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-05 | Thailand’s National Security Policy and Plan 2023–2027 incorporated changing and nontraditional security threats | Target period | 1B.1, 1B.9, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-06 | Thailand’s cyber-governance institutions were operational but evidence of independent oversight and resourcing is limited | Target-period institutional context | 1B.7, 1B.9, 1B.10 |
| WEB-1B-07 | The stalled Chinese submarine procurement created procurement, strategic-dependence, and operational-safety questions, but the submarine was conventionally powered | Inherited and target-period defence policy | 1B.1, 1B.3, 1B.5, 1B.8 |
| WEB-1B-08 | No evidence located of nuclear threats, nuclear coercion, nuclear proliferation, or nuclear-use planning by Prayut | Negative finding; target period | 1B.2, 1B.5, 1B.6, 1B.8 |
| WEB-1B-09 | Thailand’s biological-weapons and cyber positions were available through formal international processes, but implementation evidence remains thin | Target period | 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.9 |
| WEB-1B-10 | Thailand’s end-of-2023 posture was broadly stable, with incremental institutional improvements but unresolved transparency and implementation gaps | Target-period synthesis | 1B.3, 1B.7, 1B.9, 1B.10 |

## 3. Developed evidence records

### WEB-1B-01 — Non-nuclear status and inherited treaty framework

**Claim.** Thailand was not a nuclear-weapon state and had no publicly identified nuclear-weapons arsenal in 2023. Its nuclear-risk posture was based on nonproliferation, safeguards, peaceful-use commitments, and participation in regional nuclear-weapon-free-zone arrangements.

**Why it matters.** The relevant question for Prayut is not control of an existing arsenal but whether his government preserved or weakened Thailand’s nonproliferation and safeguards posture.

**Strongest source.** *Thailand Nuclear and WMD Profile*, Nuclear Threat Initiative, NTI, current country profile. The profile describes Thailand’s nuclear and WMD status, treaty participation, and civilian nuclear institutions: [NTI Thailand Nuclear and WMD Profile](https://www.nti.org/analysis/articles/thailand).

**Locator.** Country sections on nuclear capabilities, safeguards, nonproliferation, and treaties.

**Period fit.** Primarily inherited and continuing context; the page is not a contemporaneous 2023-only record.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak to moderate. Treaty status and institutional arrangements predated Prayut, but his government inherited responsibility for maintaining them.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Thailand has explored civilian nuclear technology and has military ties with nuclear-armed states. Those facts create governance responsibilities but do not demonstrate a weapons program.

**Cautions.** NTI is a specialist secondary source, not a treaty depositary or inspection record. Absence of a publicly identified arsenal is not proof that every proliferation risk was eliminated.

**Independent support.** SIPRI’s databases and arms-control materials provide independent reference coverage; IAEA and UN treaty records should be used for formal verification.

### WEB-1B-02 — Responsible multilateral nuclear rhetoric

**Claim.** In 2023, Thailand publicly presented the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons, the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons, the Comprehensive Nuclear-Test-Ban Treaty, and nuclear-weapon-free zones as complementary parts of the disarmament and nonproliferation regime.

**Why it matters.** This is direct evidence of formally responsible nuclear rhetoric: support for restraint, disarmament, verification, and regional nuclear-weapon-free arrangements rather than threats or nuclear exceptionalism.

**Strongest source.** “Thailand highlighted the complementarity of the TPNW, the nuclear weapon-free zones, and the CTBT with the NPT at the 2023 NPT Preparatory Committee in Vienna,” Permanent Mission of Thailand to the United Nations, 2023: [Permanent Mission of Thailand statement](https://permanentmission-geneva.thaiembassy.org/en/content/thailand-highlighted-the-complementarity-of-the-tp?cate=5d81d6a815e39c1a0c005773).

**Locator.** The page title and summary of Thailand’s 2023 NPT Preparatory Committee intervention.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. This was an official state position expressed through the foreign ministry’s diplomatic machinery during Prayut’s premiership. It is not evidence that Prayut personally drafted or delivered the statement.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Thailand simultaneously pursued conventional military modernization and closer defence cooperation with China. Responsible nuclear rhetoric therefore does not establish uniformly cautious security policy.

**Cautions.** Official diplomatic statements are strong evidence of formal position but may not reveal private policy preferences, implementation, or resource allocation.

**Independent support.** CTBTO and UNODA materials document Thailand’s broader support for the CTBT and NPT framework.

### WEB-1B-03 — IAEA Country Programme Framework

**Claim.** Thailand and the IAEA concluded a 2023–2029 Country Programme Framework, covering cooperation on nuclear technology and related safety and security capacities.

**Why it matters.** A formal multi-year cooperation framework indicates continued engagement with international nuclear expertise and institutional capacity-building.

**Strongest source.** “Statement by Thailand,” International Atomic Energy Agency General Conference, 2023: [IAEA Thailand General Conference statement](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/09/thailand-gc67.pdf).

**Locator.** Passage stating that Thailand had concluded the 2023–2029 Country Programme Framework and that it would be signed later in 2023.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The framework was negotiated and announced by the Thai state during Prayut’s government, but implementation involved the Office of Atoms for Peace and the IAEA rather than the prime minister personally.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** A framework is a planning and cooperation instrument, not proof that all projects were funded, staffed, or completed.

**Cautions.** The source is an official government statement delivered to the IAEA and therefore presents Thailand’s own account. Independent implementation reporting is required.

**Independent support.** The IAEA’s 2023 annual reporting and nuclear-security materials provide institutional corroboration, although the detailed country-programme documents were not separately inspected.

### WEB-1B-04 — IAEA advisory mission found a robust framework with improvements needed

**Claim.** An IAEA International Nuclear Security Advisory Service mission to Thailand in December 2023 found a robust framework for nuclear-security arrangements while recommending improvements.

**Why it matters.** This is the strongest located evidence on operational nuclear security at the end of the target year. It supports continuity and some institutional competence, but also shows that the posture was not treated as complete or risk-free.

**Strongest source.** “IAEA Mission to Thailand Finds a Robust Framework for Nuclear Security Arrangements, Encourages Improvements,” IAEA, 22 December 2023: [IAEA INSServ mission report](https://www.iaea.org/news/3171).

**Locator.** Headline and mission summary describing the robust framework and recommended improvements.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The mission assessed Thai national arrangements, not Prayut’s personal conduct. The government’s authorization and cooperation with the mission are attributable to the state during his tenure.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** “Robust framework” does not mean that every facility, source, regulation, inspection, or response capability was fully effective. The IAEA’s recommendation of improvements is material contrary evidence to any claim of complete safety.

**Cautions.** Advisory missions are cooperative and may not publish all sensitive findings. The public summary is necessarily incomplete.

**Independent support.** Thailand’s IAEA General Conference statement also claimed that the country was taking significant steps to strengthen nuclear safety and security.

### WEB-1B-05 — National Security Policy and Plan 2023–2027

**Claim.** Thailand adopted a National Security Policy and Plan for 2023–2027 framed around changing threats and sustainable security. The plan contained 17 policy areas and emphasized preparation for security crises and reduction of national-security risk.

**Why it matters.** The plan is the principal located formal policy document connecting the 2023 government to broader catastrophic and nontraditional security risks.

**Strongest source.** *Executive Summary: National Security Policy and Plan 2023–2027*, Thailand National Security Council, 2023: [Thai National Security Council executive summary](https://www.nsc.go.th/wp-content/uploads/2023/09/ExecutiveSummaryEN.pdf).

**Locator.** Executive-summary passages stating that Thailand must prepare for potential security crises, reduce national-security risks, and implement 17 policies.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for formal government policy. Prayut’s cabinet and the National Security Council were responsible for adoption and implementation during the relevant period.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The public summary does not establish budgets, staffing, independent review, measurable milestones, or successful implementation. It should not be treated as outcome evidence.

**Cautions.** The available English summary is a government translation and may omit technical detail in the full Thai plan.

**Independent support.** The National Security Council’s organizational page confirms that the plan was a 2023–2027 framework intended to coordinate agencies and implementation: [NSC organizational structure](https://www.nsc.go.th/en/organizational-structure/).

### WEB-1B-06 — Cybersecurity institutions existed, but independent capacity is difficult to verify

**Claim.** Thailand had an operating National Cyber Security Agency and a national cybersecurity governance structure in 2023. However, the inspected public material did not establish the agency’s independent oversight, budget adequacy, staffing quality, incident-response performance, or safeguards against misuse of cyber powers.

**Why it matters.** Cyber incidents can become catastrophic when they affect hospitals, critical infrastructure, financial systems, military command, or public communications. Institutional existence is not equivalent to effective risk reduction.

**Strongest source.** National Cyber Security Agency of Thailand official website: [NCSA Thailand](https://www.ncsa.or.th/home).

**Locator.** Agency homepage and institutional presentation. The page is JavaScript-rendered and provided limited extractable content.

**Period fit.** Continuing institutional evidence; exact 2023 actions were not established from the accessible page.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for the existence of the national structure; weak for Prayut-specific decisions.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The National Security Council’s 2023–2027 plan explicitly treated changing threats and crisis preparation as priorities. Thailand also participated in international cybercrime negotiations.

**Cautions.** The accessible official website was not sufficiently transparent for assessing operations, budgets, or civil-liberties safeguards. The supplied candidate cyber strategy papers were not fully inspected.

**Independent support.** The National Security Council policy framework provides context, but an independent technical audit or incident database is still needed.

### WEB-1B-07 — Chinese submarine procurement created strategic and safety questions, but was not nuclear

**Claim.** Prayut’s government continued to manage a disputed purchase of a Chinese-built S26T submarine. The originally specified German MTU engine was unavailable because of restrictions on supplying military technology to China, creating a contractual and operational impasse. Prayut publicly discussed negotiations with China in 2023. The submarine was conventionally powered, not nuclear-powered.

**Why it matters.** This was a major strategic and procurement decision under Prayut’s authority. It raises questions about military dependence, procurement scrutiny, technical assurance, and prestige-driven defence policy, but it is not evidence of nuclear capability.

**Strongest source.** “Thai Navy in talks with China to resolve submarine engine dispute: PM,” *The Nation Thailand*, 2023: [Thai Navy submarine-engine report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/policies/40026758).

**Locator.** Reported statement by Prayut that Thailand was negotiating with China over the engine and that the original delivery was expected in 2023.

**Period fit.** Target period, with inherited procurement conditions dating from 2017.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for Prayut’s role as prime minister and defence minister in handling the dispute; weaker for the original procurement decision, which predated the target year.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The procurement was a diesel-electric conventional submarine, not a nuclear submarine and not a nuclear-weapons platform. The engine problem was substantially caused by German/EU restrictions and contractual conditions, not necessarily by Thai operational negligence.

**Cautions.** Local reporting may reflect domestic political disputes and military-source claims. Procurement contracts, parliamentary approvals, audit records, and technical certification documents should be examined directly.

**Independent support.** SIPRI’s arms-transfer database provides an independent source family for major conventional arms transfers: [SIPRI databases](https://www.sipri.org/databases). Bangkok Post and specialist defence sources were identified but not opened successfully in this pass.

### WEB-1B-08 — No evidence found of nuclear threats, coercion, or proliferation

**Claim.** The inspected 2023 and retrospective sources revealed no evidence that Prayut threatened nuclear use, used nuclear capability to shield aggression or repression, directed nuclear proliferation, or authorized a nuclear-weapons program.

**Why it matters.** This is a bounded negative finding relevant to the rhetoric, escalation, and proliferation lenses.

**Evidence basis.** The official Thai 2023 NPT position emphasized disarmament, the TPNW, the CTBT, and nuclear-weapon-free zones. NTI’s country profile identifies Thailand as a non-nuclear state. The IAEA materials describe civilian nuclear safety and security cooperation rather than military nuclear activity.

**Period fit.** Target period and inherited status.

**Ruler attribution.** Limited. Absence of evidence cannot establish that Prayut personally made responsible decisions in every classified or technical domain.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** Thailand’s conventional military modernization and strategic ties with China and the United States remain relevant to regional security, but they do not constitute nuclear coercion.

**Cautions.** This is not proof of universal absence. Classified military or intelligence material could not be assessed.

### WEB-1B-09 — Biological and cyber risk positions were formally addressed but operational evidence is sparse

**Claim.** Thailand participated in international processes concerning biological weapons, cybercrime, and cyber operations in 2023. Publicly accessible material indicates formal engagement, but the inspected record does not establish the quality of domestic implementation, independent scientific advice, funding, or corrective action after incidents.

**Why it matters.** Biological and cyber risks are relevant to the “other catastrophic risks” lens even without a nuclear arsenal.

**Strongest sources.** “BWC/MSP/2023/WP.21: Statement of the Delegation of Thailand,” United Nations, 2023: [UN biological-weapons statement](https://documents.un.org/doc/undoc/gen/g23/260/67/pdf/g2326067.pdf). Thailand’s public material on cybercrime negotiations was also identified through the government public-relations portal: [Thailand cybercrime negotiation material](https://thailand.prd.go.th/en/content/category/detail/id/48/iid/253478).

**Locator.** Thailand’s delegation statement to the Biological Weapons Convention process; government material concerning international cybercrime negotiations.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for formal diplomatic positions; weak for implementation and operational conduct.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The National Security Council plan and creation of cyber institutions show that government structures existed. However, institutional existence does not demonstrate effective prevention or independent review.

**Cautions.** The UN document could not be directly fetched by the browser during this pass, though it was present in the supplied candidate catalogue and surfaced through the document URL. It should be reopened and fully extracted before being treated as final evidence. The cyber portal was also not accessible for full text.

### WEB-1B-10 — End-of-period posture: continuity with incremental improvement, not demonstrable transformation

**Claim.** By the end of 2023, Thailand’s catastrophic-risk posture appeared broadly stable: no nuclear arsenal or nuclear-use doctrine was identified; official diplomacy remained supportive of nonproliferation and disarmament; an IAEA programme framework and advisory mission indicated institutional engagement; and a new national-security plan addressed changing threats. At the same time, public evidence does not demonstrate major improvements in independent expertise, cyber safeguards, transparency, resource adequacy, or accountability.

**Why it matters.** The available evidence supports a cautious conclusion of institutional continuity and incremental strengthening, not a finding that Prayut left Thailand demonstrably safer across all catastrophic-risk domains.

**Strongest sources.** IAEA 2023 General Conference statement; IAEA December 2023 advisory-mission summary; Thailand National Security Council 2023–2027 plan; Permanent Mission of Thailand NPT PrepCom statement.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. These were state-level actions under Prayut’s government, but technical agencies and diplomatic officials carried out much of the work.

**Contrary or complicating evidence.** The IAEA’s positive description of Thailand’s nuclear-security framework is evidence of institutional strength. The absence of identified nuclear threats is also relevant, although it cannot be credited entirely to Prayut.

**Cautions.** Public information is strongest on formal commitments and weakest on budgets, staffing, classified safeguards, cyber incidents, biological preparedness, and independent correction mechanisms.

## 4. Disposition by lens

### 1B.1 — Reducing existential risk

**Disposition: Partially evidenced; no score.**

Evidence supports continued nonproliferation, IAEA cooperation, a national security plan addressing changing threats, and no nuclear escalation. Evidence is insufficient to determine whether Prayut made major resource choices specifically to reduce existential risk rather than conventional military prestige or regime security.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-07, WEB-1B-10.

### 1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric

**Disposition: Evidenced for formal diplomatic rhetoric; personal attribution limited.**

Thailand’s 2023 diplomatic position supported the NPT, TPNW, CTBT, and nuclear-weapon-free zones. No nuclear threats or reckless nuclear rhetoric were found.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-08.

### 1B.3 — Safe nuclear control

**Disposition: Partially evidenced.**

The IAEA reported a robust nuclear-security framework and recommended improvements. This supports substantial institutional capacity but does not establish the adequacy of budgets, personnel, inspections, source accounting, emergency response, or correction of every weakness.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04, WEB-1B-07, WEB-1B-10.

### 1B.4 — Arms control and inspections

**Disposition: Evidenced for formal support and international engagement; implementation incomplete.**

Thailand publicly supported the NPT, TPNW, CTBT, and nuclear-weapon-free-zone regime and maintained IAEA cooperation. Detailed 2023 safeguards and treaty-compliance records were not fully extracted.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-09.

### 1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression

**Disposition: No adverse evidence found.**

No nuclear capability, nuclear threat, or nuclear shield for conventional aggression or domestic repression was identified. Conventional military procurement should not be misclassified as nuclear conduct.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-07, WEB-1B-08.

### 1B.6 — Preventing proliferation

**Disposition: Partially evidenced.**

Thailand’s non-nuclear status and treaty framework support a nonproliferation posture. Detailed 2023 export-control enforcement, illicit-procurement investigations, and responses to proliferation alerts were not located.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-01, WEB-1B-02, WEB-1B-08.

### 1B.7 — Independent risk expertise

**Disposition: Gap.**

The IAEA relationship and Office of Atoms for Peace demonstrate access to technical institutions. The available sources do not show whether experts were independent, protected dissent, had adequate resources, or could overrule political and military preferences.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04, WEB-1B-06, WEB-1B-10.

### 1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation

**Disposition: Limited evidence.**

No nuclear crisis or nuclear escalation involving Thailand was identified. The submarine-engine dispute shows diplomatic management of a procurement problem, but it is not a nuclear crisis and provides limited evidence for catastrophic-risk decision safeguards.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-07, WEB-1B-08.

### 1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks

**Disposition: Partially evidenced; major gaps remain.**

Thailand adopted a 2023–2027 national-security framework and maintained cyber and biological diplomatic engagement. The public record inspected here does not establish effective domestic controls for cyber, biological, AI, or other dual-use risks.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-06, WEB-1B-09, WEB-1B-10.

### 1B.10 — Lasting risk posture

**Disposition: Inconclusive but broadly stable.**

The evidence indicates continuity, international engagement, and some institutional strengthening. It does not demonstrate that Prayut left Thailand’s overall catastrophic-risk posture materially safer than inherited, particularly in cyber governance, independent expertise, transparency, and implementation.

Relevant records: WEB-1B-03, WEB-1B-04, WEB-1B-05, WEB-1B-10.

## 5. Information environment and evidence limitations

Reliable information was relatively accessible for:

- treaty positions;
- IAEA cooperation;
- official national-security planning;
- the absence of a publicly declared nuclear arsenal; and
- major conventional procurement disputes.

Reliable information was much harder to obtain for:

- nuclear-security budgets and staffing;
- radioactive-source inventories and incidents;
- safeguards implementation details;
- classified military command systems;
- cyber incidents and critical-infrastructure vulnerabilities;
- biological preparedness and laboratory security;
- independent expert dissent;
- procurement oversight and technical certification.

Thai official pages were inconsistently accessible, sometimes JavaScript-rendered, unavailable in English, or blocked by cache and safety restrictions. Official diplomatic statements provide strong evidence of formal positions but are not independent proof of implementation. Defence procurement reporting is politically contested and should be checked against contracts, parliamentary records, audits, and technical documents.

The evidence also has an attribution problem: nuclear safety and security are primarily administered by specialized agencies, while Prayut’s direct authority was strongest over cabinet-level policy and defence. The record should therefore distinguish state-level continuity from personal responsibility.

- IAEA 2023 safeguards statement and country-specific safeguards material.
- IAEA nuclear-security annual review for 2023.
- Thailand’s full NCSA annual performance and incident reports.
- Parliamentary and audit records on the Chinese submarine procurement.
- Thailand’s 2023 defence budget and execution reports.
- FAS and SIPRI technical assessments of Thai military modernization.
- Academic research on Thailand’s cyber, biological, and dual-use governance.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected or successfully opened: 18.
- Sources retained as evidence records: 10 underlying facts.
- Major source families represented: IAEA, Thai government, Thai diplomatic mission, NTI, SIPRI, FAS, CTBTO, UCDP, UNICEF, WHO, and local reporting.
- Lenses with direct formal evidence: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.3, 1B.4, 1B.5, 1B.9.
- Lenses requiring substantial further work: 1B.6, 1B.7, 1B.8, and 1B.10.
- Principal unresolved issue: whether formal nuclear-safety and security commitments were matched by adequate resources, independent expertise, inspection, cyber safeguards, and correction mechanisms.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

# Chapter 2B — International Peace, Restraint, and Lawful Security

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was Thailand’s prime minister until the transfer of executive power to Srettha Thavisin in August 2023. He also held the defence portfolio during the final phase of his government. His international-security authority was substantial but constrained by the military establishment, coalition politics, the monarchy, the bureaucracy, and the transition to a caretaker government after the May election.

Thailand did not fight an interstate war in 2023 and there is no evidence that Prayut initiated an international war, territorial annexation, or cross-border military campaign. The relevant conflict exposure was:

- the inherited Malay-Muslim insurgency in Thailand’s Deep South;
- spillover risks from the Myanmar civil war;
- Thailand’s diplomatic engagement with the Myanmar junta;
- conventional military modernization and procurement; and
- accountability for security-force conduct.

The clearest positive evidence is that the government pursued a Malaysian-facilitated peace process with the BRN in the Deep South and used diplomacy rather than force in responding to Myanmar’s crisis. The clearest limitations are that the southern conflict remained unresolved, the Myanmar initiative achieved limited regional consensus, military procurement remained weakly transparent, and public evidence on civilian protection, military accountability, and remedy is incomplete.

## 2. Evidence index

|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-2B-01 | Thailand and the BRN agreed in February 2023 to develop a Joint Comprehensive Plan towards Peace | Target period | 2B.1, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-02 | The southern insurgency remained active, though violence had generally declined from earlier years | Inherited conflict and target-period outcome | 2B.2, 2B.4, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-03 | Security forces retained counterinsurgency responsibility and reliable 2023 incident reporting was limited | Target-period implementation and information gap | 2B.4, 2B.6, 2B.9 |
| WEB-2B-04 | Prayut’s caretaker government initiated informal diplomacy with Myanmar’s junta in June 2023 | Target period | 2B.1, 2B.3, 2B.7, 2B.10 |
| WEB-2B-05 | Thailand’s Myanmar diplomacy was criticized by other ASEAN governments and achieved limited success | Target-period outcome | 2B.3, 2B.6, 2B.7, 2B.10 |

| WEB-2B-11 | Thailand’s international security posture at the end of 2023 was stable but unresolved rather than demonstrably transformed | Target-period synthesis | 2B.7, 2B.8, 2B.9, 2B.10 |

## 3. Developed evidence records

### WEB-2B-01 — February 2023 peace dialogue with the BRN

**Claim.** On 21–22 February 2023, Thailand’s Peace Dialogue Panel and the Patani Malay National Revolutionary Front met in Kuala Lumpur under Malaysian facilitation. The parties agreed to develop a “Joint Comprehensive Plan towards Peace” covering violence reduction and public consultation leading toward political solutions. Technical teams were expected to draft the plan between March and May for consideration at a later formal meeting.

**Why it matters.** This is direct evidence that Prayut’s government pursued a peaceful political alternative to continued counterinsurgency operations.

**Strongest source.** “6th Meeting of the Peace Dialogue Process for Southern Border Provinces of Thailand,” Internal Security Operations Command Region 4, Royal Thai Government, 23 February 2023: [official peace-dialogue report](https://www.southpeace.go.th/?p=70308).

**Locator.** English text at lines describing the 21–22 February meeting, the JCPP, violence reduction, political consultation, and the 2023–2024 implementation timetable.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the government’s formal peace process. The initiative was conducted by the Peace Dialogue Panel and security institutions, but the source expressly states that it followed Royal Thai Government policy.

**Contrary evidence.** The source records an agreed framework, not a final settlement or verified reduction in violence. The insurgency continued.

**Cautions.** This is an official security-agency account and may emphasize progress while omitting disagreement, civilian criticism, or implementation failures.

**Independent corroboration.** Associated Press reporting describes Malaysia’s role in facilitating talks and the continuing southern insurgency: [AP report](https://apnews.com/article/ec28a896b55e42e564aa6c1d7ceff760).

### WEB-2B-02 — The southern insurgency remained unresolved

**Claim.** Thailand’s Deep South remained an active armed conflict in 2023. Violence had generally declined from earlier peaks, but attacks against civilians and security personnel continued and the conflict remained unresolved.

**Why it matters.** Prayut’s government inherited the conflict and pursued dialogue, but it did not achieve a durable ceasefire or political settlement during the target year.

**Strongest source.** RSIS’s 2023 conflict overview reports that violence had been on a downward trend since the 2013 peace dialogue but that attacks against noncombatants remained a concern. It cites Deep South Watch estimates of more than 22,100 violent incidents, approximately 7,520 deaths, and 13,900 injuries since 2004 as of August 2023: [RSIS Thailand conflict overview](https://rsis.edu.sg/ctta-newsarticle/thailand/).

**Locator.** Sections describing the declining violence trend, continued attacks, and Deep South Watch figures.

**Period fit.** Inherited conflict with target-period outcome data.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut’s government controlled security policy and the peace process, but the conflict’s origins, insurgent decisions, local grievances, and institutional history substantially predated him.

**Contrary evidence.** The downward trend and continuing dialogue are evidence against a claim that the government simply escalated the conflict without alternatives.

**Cautions.** Death and incident counts vary by dataset and definitions. Deep South Watch is a civil-society monitoring source; government compensation data and UCDP coding should be compared.

**Independent corroboration.** UCDP identifies the Thailand–Patani insurgency as a 2023 armed conflict: [UCDP 2023 conflict data](https://ucdp.uu.se/year/2023).

### WEB-2B-03 — Security-force responsibility and weak incident transparency

**Claim.** Thai military and law-enforcement bodies shared counterinsurgency responsibilities in the Deep South. The U.S. State Department’s 2023 terrorism reporting noted that insurgent violence remained concentrated in the southern provinces but that inaccurate reporting made the number of attacks and responsible parties difficult to establish.

**Why it matters.** The government had operational responsibility for security conduct, but weak or inconsistent information limits assessment of proportionality, civilian protection, and accountability.

**Strongest source.** *Country Report on Terrorism 2023: Thailand*, U.S. Department of State, 2024: [Thailand terrorism report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2119009.html).

**Locator.** Sections stating that violence remained concentrated in the southernmost provinces, that attacks likely increased slightly in 2023, that accurate reporting was difficult, and that the military and law enforcement shared counterinsurgency duties.

**Period fit.** Target-period retrospective.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for government operational responsibility; weak for individual command attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Official peace-dialogue records show that security authorities also supported negotiations and public participation.

**Cautions.** The report is a U.S. government assessment and relies on open information and partner reporting. It does not independently resolve individual allegations of abuses.

### WEB-2B-04 — Prayut’s informal diplomacy with Myanmar’s junta

**Claim.** In June 2023, Thailand’s then-caretaker government under Prayut initiated informal regional talks with Myanmar’s military authorities. The initiative sought dialogue and a possible route toward de-escalation, outside the more formal ASEAN process.

**Why it matters.** This was the most important international-security diplomatic initiative directly associated with Prayut during 2023. It shows a preference for engagement over coercion or military intervention, but also raises questions about legitimacy, inclusiveness, and whether engagement rewarded the junta.

**Strongest source.** “Why Southeast Asia Just Can’t Seem to Cut Off Myanmar’s Junta,” *TIME*, 20 October 2023: [TIME analysis](https://time.com/6326274/asean-myanmar-junta-trade/).

**Locator.** Passage stating that Thailand, under the caretaker government led by Prayuth Chan-o-cha, set up informal regional peace talks with the Myanmar junta in June 2023.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the government’s initiative; Prayut was caretaker prime minister when the talks were organized.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand’s initiative sought quiet diplomacy rather than open military support for the junta. However, the initiative did not clearly include all Myanmar stakeholders and was criticized by other ASEAN states.

**Cautions.** TIME’s account is analytical reporting rather than a treaty or formal diplomatic record. The full participant list, agenda, and outcomes require official minutes or diplomatic cables.

**Independent corroboration.** Associated Press reporting records regional disagreement over how ASEAN should respond to Myanmar and the appeal of dialogue and humanitarian assistance: [AP ASEAN report](https://apnews.com/article/07ff4443471fae04c2d093608d4eeb70).

### WEB-2B-05 — Limited success and ASEAN disagreement over Myanmar policy

**Claim.** Thailand’s Myanmar initiative was not supported by a unified ASEAN position. More critical ASEAN governments viewed Thailand’s quiet diplomacy with caution, and the initiative achieved limited success against a continuing civil war.

**Why it matters.** Pursuing dialogue is evidence of peaceful alternatives, but the method may have reduced regional unity if it was perceived as legitimizing the junta or bypassing ASEAN’s agreed Five-Point Consensus.

**Strongest source.** TIME’s regional analysis states that Thailand and Indonesia pursued quiet diplomacy with Myanmar, that Thailand’s June talks were “snubbed” by more critical ASEAN states, and that ASEAN’s response remained weak: [TIME Myanmar analysis](https://time.com/6326274/asean-myanmar-junta-trade/).

**Locator.** Passages on Thailand’s June 2023 talks, ASEAN division, and the limited success of quiet diplomacy.

**Period fit.** Target-period retrospective.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong. The initiative was conducted under Prayut’s caretaker government, though Thailand’s foreign ministry and senior diplomats were operationally responsible.

**Contrary evidence.** Engagement can preserve communication channels and create access for humanitarian diplomacy. The evidence does not establish that Thailand intended to undermine ASEAN or assist military abuses.

**Cautions.** The source is a regional analysis and reflects interpretation of diplomatic motives. Direct records of Thai assurances to the junta and other ASEAN states are needed.

### WEB-2B-06 — Myanmar created a severe humanitarian and border-security environment

**Claim.** Myanmar’s civil war and military repression generated major humanitarian and security pressures during 2023. In February, the UN reported 17.6 million people in humanitarian need, more than 1.5 million internally displaced people, continuing airstrikes and fighting, and severe restrictions on humanitarian access.

**Why it matters.** Thailand’s Myanmar policy operated under genuine external humanitarian and border-security constraints. These conditions help explain the government’s preference for engagement but also create a responsibility to protect refugees and avoid enabling abuses.

**Strongest source.** *Myanmar Humanitarian Update No. 26*, UN OCHA, 2 February 2023: [UN Myanmar humanitarian update](https://myanmar.un.org/en/217948-myanmar-humanitarian-update-no-26).

**Locator.** Highlights and situation overview reporting humanitarian need, displacement, airstrikes, access restrictions, and protection risks.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Weak for the Myanmar conflict itself; moderate for Thailand’s response to the resulting regional crisis.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand had practical reasons to maintain working relations with Myanmar’s authorities and border actors, including humanitarian access, refugee management, and cross-border security.

**Cautions.** The source describes Myanmar-wide conditions, not Thailand’s conduct. Separate evidence is required on Thai border closures, refugee admissions, returns, aid access, and military contacts.

### WEB-2B-07 — Military expenditure and modernization

**Claim.** Thailand’s military expenditure was approximately $5.7 billion in 2022, about 1.2% of GDP, according to SIPRI’s Thailand budget reporting. Specialist analysis characterizes Thai procurement during the Prayut period as modernization rather than an alarming regional arms build-up.

**Why it matters.** Military resources should be assessed for genuine security needs, proportionality, transparency, and institutional entrenchment.

**Strongest source.** SIPRI, *Thailand, 2023*, National Budget Archive: [SIPRI Thailand 2023 budget report](https://www.sipri.org/national-budget-report/thailand/2023). Supporting specialist analysis: [National Institute for Defense Studies, Thailand chapter](https://www.nids.mod.go.jp/english/publication/joint_research/series13/pdf/07.pdf).

**Locator.** SIPRI Thailand 2023 country page; NIDS discussion of Thai procurement and modernization.

**Period fit.** SIPRI’s figures primarily describe the 2023 budget reporting cycle; the NIDS analysis covers the broader Prayut period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut led the military-backed government and served as defence minister, but procurement decisions were strongly shaped by the armed forces and bureaucratic institutions.

**Contrary evidence.** NIDS does not characterize the procurement pattern as an alarming arms build-up. Military expenditure was moderate by regional and global standards.

**Cautions.** Military-spending estimates differ from appropriated and executed budgets. The SIPRI figure is not proof that individual procurement projects were necessary or honestly administered.

### WEB-2B-08 — Chinese submarine procurement and accountability concerns

**Claim.** The Chinese S26T submarine procurement, initiated before 2023, remained stalled because Germany would not supply the specified MTU engine for the Chinese-built vessel. In 2023 Prayut publicly managed negotiations over the dispute. The episode raised questions about procurement transparency, strategic dependence on China, technical assurance, and whether the acquisition reflected military institutional preference more than a demonstrated operational need.

**Why it matters.** This is a major example of military-resource allocation and civil-military influence. It is relevant to responsible security resources even though the submarine was conventionally powered and not connected to nuclear weapons.

**Strongest source.** “Thai Navy in talks with China to resolve submarine engine dispute: PM,” *The Nation Thailand*, 2023: [submarine-engine report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/policies/40026758).

**Locator.** Reported statement by Prayut that Thailand was negotiating with China and that the vessel had originally been expected in 2023.

**Period fit.** Inherited procurement with target-period management.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for Prayut’s role in handling the dispute as prime minister and defence minister; weaker for the original acquisition decision.

**Contrary evidence.** The procurement was a conventional diesel-electric submarine. The engine problem was partly caused by German and EU restrictions on military technology exports to China, not solely by Thai misconduct.

**Cautions.** Local reporting may reflect domestic political and military-source disputes. The contract, parliamentary approval, audit findings, and technical certification should be inspected directly.

**Independent corroboration.** The Journal of Indo-Pacific Affairs discusses the purchase, civil-military procurement dynamics, and concerns about transparency and quality control: [JIPA analysis](https://www.airuniversity.af.edu/JIPA/Display/Article/3606756/hedging-alignment-and-unintended-consequences-the-geopolitical-meaning-and-outc/).

### WEB-2B-09 — No evidence of initiating interstate war

**Claim.** No inspected source identifies Prayut’s government as initiating an interstate war, annexing territory, conducting a sustained cross-border military campaign, or using proxy forces against a foreign state in 2023.

**Why it matters.** The conflict-exposure gate is important: Thailand’s main armed conflict was an internal insurgency, while Myanmar presented a diplomatic and humanitarian crisis rather than an interstate war between Thailand and Myanmar.

**Evidence basis.** UCDP’s 2023 conflict material identifies the Thailand–Patani insurgency; the Myanmar sources describe cross-border humanitarian pressure and diplomacy, not Thai military intervention. The available Thailand reporting does not identify a Thai interstate war.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Negative evidence only. It cannot prove that no classified or limited cross-border operation occurred.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand has experienced border incidents and military tensions in previous years, including spillover from Myanmar, but these were not established here as a 2023 war initiated by Prayut.

**Cautions.** Negative findings require careful limits. Border patrol incidents, covert assistance, refugee pushbacks, and intelligence cooperation need separate records.

### WEB-2B-10 — Civilian protection, investigations, and remedy remain insufficiently documented

**Claim.** The accessible 2023 evidence does not establish a complete record of Thai rules of engagement, civilian-harm investigations, prosecutions of security personnel, casualty disclosure, or effective remedies for unlawful conduct in the Deep South.

**Why it matters.** Peace dialogue and declining violence do not by themselves demonstrate lawful conduct or accountability.

**Evidence basis.** The U.S. State Department report notes difficulty obtaining accurate incident information. The official peace-dialogue report focuses on process and does not provide civilian-harm or accountability data. Deep South Watch and RSIS provide violence figures but not a complete legal-accountability record.

**Period fit.** Target-period evidence gap with inherited accountability implications.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate for institutional responsibility; insufficient for personal attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand maintained formal security institutions, compensation mechanisms, and a peace dialogue process. These may provide avenues for remedy, but their actual effectiveness was not established here.

**Cautions.** This is a gap finding, not a finding that no investigations or remedies existed.

### WEB-2B-11 — End-of-period international-security posture

**Claim.** At the end of 2023, Thailand’s international-security posture was more accurately described as stable but unresolved than as either escalatory or decisively pacifying. The government avoided interstate war, pursued southern peace talks, and attempted Myanmar engagement, but neither conflict was durably settled. Military modernization and procurement continued, while transparency and accountability remained incomplete.

**Why it matters.** This is the appropriate period-level synthesis without assigning a score.

**Strongest sources.** Official Thai peace-dialogue report; TIME Myanmar analysis; UCDP; SIPRI; U.S. State Department terrorism report.

**Period fit.** Target-period synthesis.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut had meaningful authority over diplomacy, defence, and security policy but shared responsibility with military institutions, diplomats, coalition partners, Malaysia, ASEAN, insurgent organizations, and Myanmar’s military authorities.

**Contrary evidence.** The peace process and quiet diplomacy were concrete nonviolent initiatives. The absence of an interstate war is also material positive evidence.

**Cautions.** The end-state cannot be measured solely by 2023 events; subsequent implementation of the JCPP and Myanmar diplomacy should be checked.

## 4. Disposition by lens

### 2B.1 — Peaceful alternatives

**Disposition: Evidenced, with mixed implementation.**

The government pursued the BRN dialogue and attempted quiet diplomacy with Myanmar. These were genuine peaceful alternatives, though the evidence does not establish that all feasible alternatives were explored or that the Myanmar process was sufficiently inclusive.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-06.

### 2B.2 — Starting or prolonging war

**Disposition: No evidence of initiating an interstate war; inherited internal conflict remained unresolved.**

No Thai interstate war or annexation was identified. The southern insurgency continued, but it substantially predated Prayut and the government pursued negotiations alongside security operations.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-09.

### 2B.3 — Honest justification for force

**Disposition: Incomplete evidence.**

No major 2023 Thai interstate use of force requiring a public justification was identified. For the Deep South, the government framed security policy around public safety and national security, but the accessible record does not permit a full assessment of threat evidence, alternatives, or whether official claims were consistently accurate.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-03, WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-09.

### 2B.4 — Civilian and prisoner protection

**Disposition: Major evidence gap.**

The southern conflict exposed civilians to insurgent and state-security risks. Publicly accessible sources do not provide a complete 2023 record of rules of engagement, prisoner treatment, investigations, or remedy.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-03, WEB-2B-06, WEB-2B-10.

### 2B.5 — Control of proxies and arms

**Disposition: Little direct evidence of foreign proxy support; conventional procurement requires further inspection.**

No evidence was found that Prayut supplied foreign proxies to conduct abuses. The Chinese submarine and broader military procurement raise arms-control and transparency questions but do not establish proxy abuse.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-07, WEB-2B-08, WEB-2B-09.

### 2B.6 — Scrutiny of war claims

**Disposition: Partially evidenced, with significant transparency limitations.**

UCDP, Deep South Watch, RSIS, international reporting, and U.S. government reporting provided independent checks. However, Thai security reporting and incident data remained incomplete and politically sensitive.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-03, WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-10.

### 2B.7 — Ceasefires and settlements

**Disposition: Peace efforts evidenced; durable settlement not achieved.**

The JCPP process was an important 2023 initiative, but the evidence does not show a completed ceasefire, signed settlement, or durable implementation.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-11.

### 2B.8 — Responsible military resources

**Disposition: Mixed and unresolved.**

SIPRI and specialist analysis suggest modernization rather than an obvious regional arms race. However, the submarine procurement and broader military autonomy raised concerns about transparency, institutional interests, and technical justification.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-07, WEB-2B-08.

### 2B.9 — Accountability and remedy

**Disposition: Major evidence gap.**

The inspected record does not establish whether Prayut’s government systematically investigated unlawful security-force conduct, disclosed casualties, disciplined responsible actors, or provided meaningful remedies. Procurement accountability also requires direct audit and parliamentary records.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-03, WEB-2B-08, WEB-2B-10.

### 2B.10 — Lasting international peace

**Disposition: Inconclusive, with limited positive movement.**

Thailand avoided interstate war and advanced two diplomatic tracks, but the southern conflict remained unresolved and Myanmar diplomacy produced limited regional consensus. No durable settlement or clearly safer end-state was demonstrated by the end of 2023.

Relevant records: WEB-2B-01, WEB-2B-02, WEB-2B-04, WEB-2B-05, WEB-2B-11.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Information was strongest for:

- formal peace-dialogue meetings;
- official diplomatic initiatives;
- broad conflict trends;
- Myanmar’s humanitarian conditions; and
- military-spending estimates.

Information was weaker for:

- civilian casualties and security-force abuses;
- prisoner treatment;
- rules of engagement;
- secret or informal military cooperation with Myanmar;
- Thai border practices toward refugees;
- procurement contracts and technical assessments;
- investigations and remedies;
- the private objectives of Thailand’s Myanmar diplomacy.

Official Thai sources document policy and formal commitments well but tend to emphasize progress and national-security framing. International and civil-society sources provide useful checks but may have limited access to conflict areas. Conflict counts differ substantially by source, and the U.S. State Department specifically noted reporting limitations.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

# Chapter 3B — Domestic Safety, Restraint, and Protection

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was prime minister until the formation of Srettha Thavisin’s government in August 2023. During the relevant period, Prayut’s government retained substantial control over police, military, internal-security, and detention institutions, although practical authority was shared with the military hierarchy, courts, prosecutors, and provincial security bodies.

Thailand’s 2023 physical-safety environment combined:

- continuing political detention and protest-related prosecutions;
- allegations and documented patterns of torture, enforced disappearance, and excessive force;
- an unresolved insurgency in the southern border provinces;
- refugee and migrant detention risks;
- a new torture-and-enforced-disappearance law whose key safeguards were temporarily delayed by the cabinet and then restored by the Constitutional Court; and
- limited accountability for earlier and continuing abuses.

There is positive evidence of legal reform, court intervention, some compensation, and reduced violence in parts of the Deep South. However, the overall record indicates that Prayut’s government did not establish reliable protection from state abuse. It continued to use coercive legal and security tools against dissent, delayed important detention safeguards, and failed to resolve longstanding impunity concerns.

## 2. Evidence index
