## 3. Developed evidence records

### WEB-3B-01 — Torture and Enforced Disappearance Act entered into force

**Claim.** Thailand’s Prevention and Suppression of Torture and Enforced Disappearance Act entered into force on 22 February 2023. It criminalized torture, ill-treatment, and enforced disappearance and created procedural safeguards intended to help victims report abuse and obtain redress.

**Why it matters.** This was the most important formal protection measure during the target period and addressed long-standing gaps in Thai law.

**Strongest source.** “Thailand: Law to address torture and enforced disappearance is an important step toward justice but its full enforcement is critical,” Amnesty International Thailand and the International Commission of Jurists, 22 February 2023: [Amnesty/ICJ statement](https://www.amnesty.or.th/en/blog/2023/02/thailand-law-to-address-torture-and-enforced-disappearance-is-an-important-step-toward-justice-but-its-full-enforcement-is-critical/).

**Locator.** Sections stating that the law came into force on 22 February 2023, criminalized torture and disappearance, and established safeguards and reporting mechanisms.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for formal cabinet-level implementation conditions. The law resulted from parliamentary and civil-society efforts over many years, so personal authorship cannot be attributed to Prayut.

**Contrary evidence.** The same source identifies legal shortcomings and delayed enforcement of key provisions. Entry into force therefore did not itself prove effective protection.

**Cautions.** Amnesty and ICJ are rights-monitoring organizations and emphasize implementation risks. Their legal account is strong, but outcome claims require official enforcement data.

**Independent corroboration.** The UN Committee Against Torture recognized the Act as a significant legal development: [Thailand’s UN Committee Against Torture report](https://documents.un.org/doc/undoc/gen/g24/203/16/pdf/g2420316.pdf).

### WEB-3B-02 — Cabinet delayed core detention safeguards

**Claim.** On 14 February 2023, Prayut’s cabinet postponed enforcement of sections 22–25 of the new Act until 1 October 2023. These provisions required continuous audio-video recording of arrest and detention, recording of detainee information and condition, and notification or access to information for relatives and lawyers. The government cited shortages of cameras, equipment, training, and personnel.

**Why it matters.** The delayed provisions directly addressed risks of torture, disappearance, incommunicado detention, and fabricated or concealed detention records.

**Strongest source.** Amnesty International Thailand, 21 February 2023: [Amnesty statement on the postponement](https://www.amnesty.or.th/en/blog/2023/02/amnesty-international-thailand-handing-letter-to-ministry-of-justice-demanding-that-the-act-on-prevention-and-suppression-of-torture-and-enforced-disappearance-be-enforced-in-its-entirety/).

**Locator.** Sections describing the cabinet decision, the stated resource and training reasons, and the content of sections 22–25.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. The decision was made by Prayut’s cabinet through an executive decree.

**Contrary evidence.** The government’s explanation identified genuine operational prerequisites: equipment, staffing, and training. The cabinet did not repeal the law and claimed the delay was needed to make implementation workable.

**Cautions.** Amnesty is critical of the decision and characterizes the legal basis as inappropriate. The cabinet’s underlying equipment and procurement records were not inspected.

### WEB-3B-03 — Constitutional Court restored the safeguards

**Claim.** On 18 May 2023, the Constitutional Court invalidated the decree delaying sections 22–25 and held that the safeguards were effective retroactively from 22 February. The provisions required continuous recording, detention records, and notification of relatives or lawyers.

**Why it matters.** This demonstrates that an independent judicial institution constrained the executive and restored safeguards against detention abuse.

**Strongest source.** “Court annuls decree delaying enforcement of torture, disappearance law,” *The Nation Thailand*, 18 May 2023: [court report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/general/40027769).

**Locator.** Reported 8–1 Constitutional Court decision, retroactive effect, and description of sections 22–25.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the cabinet’s original delay; moderate for the corrective outcome, which was imposed by the court rather than voluntarily adopted by Prayut.

**Contrary evidence.** The court’s intervention provided an important institutional remedy. It does not prove that police, military, and other officials immediately implemented the safeguards effectively.

**Cautions.** The source is reputable local reporting rather than the full Constitutional Court judgment. The judgment should be obtained for authoritative legal analysis.

### WEB-3B-04 — Torture and disappearance allegations remained unresolved

**Claim.** Human-rights organizations continued to receive allegations of torture, ill-treatment, and enforced disappearance by security forces, particularly in the southern border provinces. Amnesty and ICJ reported that, as of their 2023 statement, many disappearance cases remained unresolved and prosecutions and reparations were limited.

**Why it matters.** The evidence indicates that the new law addressed an entrenched pattern rather than an entirely prospective risk. Effective protection required investigations, disclosure, prosecution, and remedy.

**Strongest source.** Amnesty/ICJ statement, 22 February 2023: [source](https://www.amnesty.or.th/en/blog/2023/02/thailand-law-to-address-torture-and-enforced-disappearance-is-an-important-step-toward-justice-but-its-full-enforcement-is-critical/).

**Locator.** Sections reporting complaints from the National Human Rights Commission and southern civil-society organizations, unresolved disappearance cases, low prosecution rates, and incomplete reparations.

**Period fit.** Inherited pattern documented during the target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong institutionally. Prayut’s government controlled the security and justice institutions responsible for prevention and investigation, but many cases predated his 2023 tenure.

**Contrary evidence.** The new law and the Constitutional Court ruling created stronger formal tools. A small number of high-profile prosecutions also show that accountability was not entirely absent.

**Cautions.** NGO estimates and case counts require comparison with official records and UN Working Group data. Unresolved allegations should not be treated as proven individual crimes without case-specific findings.

### WEB-3B-05 — Political detention and prosecution continued

**Claim.** Thai Lawyers for Human Rights reported that at least 67 people were detained as political prisoners during 2023, with lèse-majesté the most common charge. Human Rights Watch reported at least 258 people prosecuted in 2023 in connection with democracy protests or social-media activity under lèse-majesté provisions. Some defendants were held in pretrial detention or released only under restrictive conditions.

**Why it matters.** Political imprisonment and prolonged pretrial detention directly affected physical safety, bodily autonomy, and protection from arbitrary punishment.

**Strongest sources.** Thai Lawyers for Human Rights, *Overview of 2023 for political prisoners*: [TLHR 2023 overview](https://tlhr2014.com/en/archives/64728). Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** TLHR’s 2023 detention totals; HRW sections on prosecutions, pretrial detention, bail restrictions, and continued use of article 112.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the continuation of the legal and prosecutorial system under Prayut until August; weaker for cases initiated or continued under the successor government.

**Contrary evidence.** Courts sometimes granted bail, and the 2023 election permitted opposition campaigning. These facts show that the state was not uniformly closed or violent.

**Cautions.** TLHR is a legal-aid and monitoring organization with a rights-protection perspective. Its figures are valuable but should be checked against court registers.

### WEB-3B-06 — Protest-force accountability remained weak

**Claim.** Human Rights Watch reported little progress in cases alleging excessive force by riot police during democracy protests from 2020 through 2023. It also recorded a 2023 Civil Court order requiring police to pay compensation to activist Tanat Thanakitamnuay, who lost sight in one eye after being struck by a police-fired tear-gas canister during a 2021 protest.

**Why it matters.** The compensation order demonstrates a judicial remedy, but the broader absence of criminal accountability indicates weak control and discipline of coercive forces.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** “Lack of Accountability for State-Sponsored Abuses,” including the 22 August 2023 Civil Court order and lack of progress in other protest-force cases.

**Period fit.** Target-period judicial outcome concerning inherited conduct.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Police command and prosecutorial institutions operated under the government, but the specific 2021 incident preceded the target year and individual officers require case-specific attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** The Civil Court’s compensation order shows that judicial review could produce a remedy.

**Cautions.** HRW summarizes litigation and monitoring; the full judgment and police disciplinary files should be inspected.

### WEB-3B-07 — Southern violence declined early and increased later

**Claim.** Human Rights Watch reported that violence in the southern border provinces subsided during the first half of 2023 after Thai military and BRN representatives announced efforts to reduce violence during Ramadan. Insurgent attacks on military targets and civilians increased after August despite continuing dialogue.

**Why it matters.** This provides a mixed safety outcome: partial short-term reduction, followed by renewed insecurity, with no durable settlement.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** “Violence and Abuses in the Southern Border Provinces,” describing first-half reduction and post-August increase.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut’s government controlled counterinsurgency and dialogue policy until the August transition, but insurgent choices and local actors were independent causes.

**Contrary evidence.** The decline during Ramadan and peace dialogue indicate that de-escalation was possible and that government policy was not exclusively coercive.

**Cautions.** HRW’s account should be reconciled with UCDP and Deep South Watch event counts.

### WEB-3B-08 — Civilian exposure to state and insurgent violence

**Claim.** The southern conflict exposed civilians to both insurgent violence and security-force abuse. Human Rights Watch reported more than 7,000 deaths since 2004, continuing attacks on civilians, and allegations that Thai security personnel tortured, unlawfully killed, or otherwise abused Malay-Muslim residents. It also reported that BRN recruitment of children continued.

**Why it matters.** Domestic safety must account for both non-state and state violence. A reduction in insurgent attacks does not offset unremedied abuse by government forces.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** Sections on torture, enforced disappearance, and violence and abuses in the southern border provinces.

**Period fit.** Inherited conflict with target-period observations.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The government was responsible for its own forces and for the legal framework in which they operated; it was not responsible for insurgent decisions except insofar as policy affected foreseeable consequences.

**Contrary evidence.** Peace dialogue, reduced violence in early 2023, and compensation mechanisms show some protective or remedial activity.

**Cautions.** The aggregate death toll spans nearly two decades and cannot be attributed to Prayut or 2023 alone.

### WEB-3B-09 — Refugees, asylum seekers, migrants, and dissidents faced protection risks

**Claim.** Human Rights Watch reported that Thailand was not party to the Refugee Convention, continued to treat many asylum seekers as illegal migrants, and had returned or deported people to countries where they might face persecution or ill-treatment. It also reported long-term immigration detention of Uyghurs and Rohingya and the 2023 deaths of two Uyghur detainees in Bangkok.

**Why it matters.** Protection from physical harm extends to people under state custody and to vulnerable non-citizens. Detention conditions and refoulement create direct safety risks.

**Strongest source.** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** “Refugees, Asylum Seekers, and Migrant Workers,” including detention, refoulement, dissident abduction, and deaths in immigration detention.

**Period fit.** Target period, with some broader 2014–2023 context.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Immigration and detention systems were national institutions under Prayut’s executive authority, though individual removals and foreign-state conduct require separate attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** HRW records that Thailand hosted large numbers of refugees and that the government launched a National Screening Mechanism in September 2023, although implementation details were unclear.

**Cautions.** Detention and refoulement claims should be checked against immigration records, UNHCR documentation, and individual case files.

### WEB-3B-10 — Independent scrutiny existed but remained constrained

**Claim.** Independent organizations, courts, the National Human Rights Commission, UN mechanisms, and legal-aid groups documented abuses and challenged government conduct. At the same time, activists and journalists faced intimidation, criminal prosecution, surveillance, and restrictions that limited reporting and organizing.

**Why it matters.** The ability to report abuse and obtain independent review is itself a condition of domestic safety. Thailand had meaningful monitoring capacity but not a consistently safe or independent environment for complainants and investigators.

**Strongest sources.** Freedom House, *Freedom in the World 2023: Thailand*: [Freedom House report](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand/freedom-world/2023). Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** Freedom House sections on arbitrary arrests, intimidation, impunity, spyware, and emergency powers; HRW sections on defenders, prosecutions, and accountability.

**Period fit.** Inherited and target-period environment.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the national legal and security environment; individual surveillance and intimidation incidents require separate proof.

**Contrary evidence.** The existence of public monitoring, court review, and civil-society reporting demonstrates that scrutiny was not eliminated.

**Cautions.** International reports may overrepresent prominent Bangkok cases and undercount rural or minority victims.

### WEB-3B-11 — Legal improvement did not produce durable protection

**Claim.** By the end of 2023, Thailand had enacted a significant anti-torture and enforced-disappearance law and the Constitutional Court had restored its key safeguards, but political detention, protest prosecutions, southern abuses, refugee-protection failures, and weak accountability continued. The available evidence therefore supports formal improvement without a demonstrated durable improvement in physical safety.

**Why it matters.** This is the period-level synthesis: legal reform was meaningful, but implementation and remedy remained inadequate.

**Strongest sources.** Amnesty/ICJ legal assessment; Constitutional Court reporting; HRW 2024 Thailand chapter; TLHR 2023 detention monitoring.

**Period fit.** Target-period synthesis.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the cabinet’s decision to delay safeguards and for the broader security environment under Prayut; weaker for inherited cases and post-August developments.

**Contrary evidence.** Court correction, legal reform, compensation, and partial reduction of southern violence show institutional capacity for improvement.

**Cautions.** The successor government inherited and continued many cases, so the complete 2023 outcome cannot be attributed to Prayut alone.

## 4. Disposition by lens

### 3B.1 — Protection from state abuse

**Disposition: Adverse evidence with formal legal improvement.**

The new Act was a significant protection, but allegations of torture, disappearance, political imprisonment, and inadequate remedy persisted. Prayut’s cabinet delayed the Act’s core detention safeguards before the Constitutional Court restored them.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-01, WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.2 — Control of coercive forces

**Disposition: Incomplete and adverse.**

Security forces retained broad detention and counterinsurgency powers. Human-rights reporting identified torture, excessive force, and weak disciplinary or criminal accountability. The record does not establish a consistent national command policy ordering abuse, but it supports a pattern of inadequate prevention and remedy.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-08.

### 3B.3 — Incitement and targeted hatred

**Disposition: Limited direct evidence; no strong finding.**

The inspected sources document repression, legal targeting, and security abuse but do not establish that Prayut personally or officially incited hatred or physical violence against a protected group. The Deep South involved ethnic and religious conflict, but attribution of incitement requires more specific speeches and orders.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-08, WEB-3B-10.

### 3B.4 — Complaints and independent oversight

**Disposition: Mixed.**

The courts, National Human Rights Commission, UN mechanisms, and civil-society organizations provided meaningful scrutiny. The Constitutional Court reversed the cabinet’s delay, and the Civil Court awarded compensation in a protest-injury case. However, many investigations and prosecutions remained stalled or ineffective.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-10.

### 3B.5 — Emergency and surveillance powers

**Disposition: Adverse, with some judicial correction.**

The cabinet used an emergency decree to postpone detention safeguards, and the broader security system relied on exceptional powers in the Deep South. Protest-related emergency prosecutions and surveillance concerns continued. The Constitutional Court later invalidated the postponement decree.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-10.

### 3B.6 — Safety without state terror

**Disposition: Mixed but unresolved.**

Southern violence declined in the first half of 2023 but increased later. The government avoided evidence of generalized state terror, yet allegations of torture, disappearance, excessive force, and political detention created a broader climate of fear for activists and minorities.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-04, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-08.

### 3B.7 — Protection of vulnerable groups

**Disposition: Inadequate and uneven.**

Malay-Muslim communities, refugees, asylum seekers, migrants, children involved in protests, and detainees faced significant risks. The National Screening Mechanism was a possible positive development, but implementation evidence was limited.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-08, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.8 — Safe protest and dissent

**Disposition: Adverse.**

Political prosecutions, restrictive bail conditions, pretrial detention, surveillance, intimidation, and excessive police force limited the physical safety of protesters and organizers.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-10.

### 3B.9 — Restrained crisis response

**Disposition: Mixed and contested.**

The government pursued legal reform and some dialogue in the Deep South, but its decision to delay detention safeguards invoked emergency powers for administrative and resource reasons. Protest policing and detention practices remained coercive, and accountability was weak.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-02, WEB-3B-03, WEB-3B-05, WEB-3B-06, WEB-3B-09.

### 3B.10 — Lasting domestic safety

**Disposition: Inconclusive, with no demonstrated durable improvement.**

There were meaningful legal reforms and some decline in southern violence, but no evidence that Thailand ended the inherited patterns of state abuse, political detention, impunity, or insecurity for vulnerable non-citizens. The end-of-period posture was legally improved but practically fragile.

Relevant records: WEB-3B-07, WEB-3B-08, WEB-3B-09, WEB-3B-11.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Reliable information was relatively accessible for:

- the text and timing of the anti-torture law;
- the cabinet’s postponement decree;
- the Constitutional Court’s intervention;
- political-detention monitoring;
- public protest prosecutions; and
- broad southern-conflict trends.

Information was weaker for:

- secret detention and interrogation;
- police and military command orders;
- civilian casualty attribution;
- prison and immigration-detention conditions;
- disciplinary outcomes for security personnel;
- refugee pushbacks and informal returns;
- surveillance authorization and technical scope; and
- local abuses outside Bangkok.

The information environment was itself part of the safety problem. Activists, journalists, lawyers, and victims faced intimidation, prosecution, surveillance, and possible retaliation. Official statistics and security accounts therefore require comparison with court records, NGO case files, UN mechanisms, and local-language reporting.

### Promising leads still needing inspection

- National Human Rights Commission of Thailand’s 2023 civil-and-political-rights report.

- Thai Lawyers for Human Rights’ full 2023 political-prisoner dataset.
- Court files concerning the Constitutional Court’s anti-torture-law ruling.
- Police procurement and training records for mandatory detention recordings.
- Full OHCHR communications on torture, non-refoulement, and protest policing.
- Amnesty’s Tak Bai accountability report.
- Deep South Watch’s 2023 incident database.
- Immigration detention and refugee-return records.
- Official police disciplinary and prosecution records concerning protest-force cases.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected or successfully opened: 21.
- Accepted evidence records: 11 underlying facts.
- Strongest evidence: legal reform, cabinet delay, court correction, political detention, and broad patterns of impunity.
- Main unresolved questions: actual implementation of the anti-torture law, detention-record compliance, the scale of 2023 torture and disappearance, police discipline, refugee-return practices, and national command responsibility for local abuses.
- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

# Chapter 4B — Political Freedom versus Authoritarian Rule

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha governed Thailand as prime minister until August 2023. His government operated under the 2017 constitution drafted after the 2014 military coup. The constitution permitted an appointed Senate to participate in selecting the prime minister, and the Senate remained a decisive institutional constraint on electoral alternation.

Thailand’s May 2023 election was genuinely competitive in important respects. Move Forward won the largest number of seats and more than 14 million votes. However, the constitutional system allowed the military-appointed Senate and allied parliamentary actors to prevent Move Forward from forming a government. Pheu Thai later formed a coalition including parties associated with Prayut’s previous government, and Srettha Thavisin became prime minister.

The period therefore shows a dual reality:

- electoral competition and public opposition were real and consequential;
- the constitutional and institutional order preserved strong military-monarchical influence;
- dissent, protest, journalism, and online expression remained subject to criminalization, surveillance, and self-censorship; and
- independent institutions were formally active but often politically contested or vulnerable to elite influence.

Prayut did not remain in office after the 2023 election, but the institutional framework created and maintained during his rule limited the extent to which the election could produce a normal democratic transfer.

## 2. Evidence index

| ID | Underlying fact | Period/status | Lenses |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEB-4B-01 | The 2023 election was competitive and Move Forward won the largest vote and seat share | Target period | 4B.1, 4B.8, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-02 | The military-appointed Senate helped prevent Move Forward from forming a government | Target period | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-03 | The election observer record identified accountability, transparency, and inclusiveness but also recommended institutional reforms | Target period | 4B.1, 4B.4, 4B.10 |
| WEB-4B-04 | The constitutional order entrenched military influence over executive formation | Inherited and target-period institutional condition | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-05 | Political prosecutions, pretrial detention, and bail restrictions continued | Target period | 4B.3, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-06 | Lèse-majesté and related laws restricted criticism and political organization | Target period and inherited legal framework | 4B.3, 4B.6, 4B.7 |

| WEB-4B-09 | Courts and election institutions were active but their independence and political neutrality were contested | Target period | 4B.4, 4B.5, 4B.8 |
| WEB-4B-10 | The end-of-period democratic posture was competitive but structurally authoritarian and not durably liberalized | Target-period synthesis | 4B.1, 4B.2, 4B.4, 4B.10 |

## 3. Developed evidence records

### WEB-4B-01 — Competitive election and opposition victory

**Claim.** Thailand’s 14 May 2023 general election was competitive and produced a decisive opposition result. Move Forward won 151 seats and more than 14 million votes, becoming the largest party. Prayut’s United Thai Nation Party won 36 seats.

**Why it matters.** The election demonstrates that voters could express a meaningful preference against the military-backed governing order.

**Strongest sources.** Asian Network for Free Elections, *2023 Thai General Election: Democracy at a Crossroads*, released 15 August 2023: [ANFREL final-report announcement](https://anfrel.org/2023-thai-general-election-democracy-at-a-crossroads/). Freedom House’s *Freedom on the Net 2023* also records that Move Forward won the election and received more than 14 million votes: [Freedom on the Net Thailand 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand/freedom-net/2023).

**Locator.** ANFREL announcement describing the final observation findings; Freedom House overview, passages on the 2023 election and Move Forward’s vote.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut was the incumbent prime minister and his party competed in the election, but the vote was administered by the Election Commission and shaped by voters, parties, courts, and the constitutional system.

**Contrary evidence.** The election framework was not fully level: the appointed Senate retained a role in choosing the prime minister, and media, legal, and institutional conditions favored established actors.

**Cautions.** ANFREL’s full PDF was not machine-readable in the accessible browser, so the announcement page was used for the principal claim. Official final results should be retained for exact vote and seat totals.

### WEB-4B-02 — Senate obstruction of the electoral transfer

**Claim.** Move Forward’s electoral victory did not produce government formation. The military-appointed Senate and allied parliamentary actors opposed Pita Limjaroenrat’s candidacy, and the party was unable to secure the combined parliamentary majority required by the constitution. Pheu Thai later formed a coalition including former military-aligned parties.

**Why it matters.** This is the central evidence that electoral competition existed within an institutional system designed to preserve military influence over executive succession.

**Strongest sources.** Freedom House country profile: [Thailand country profile](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand). Reporters Without Borders, *Thailand 2023*: [RSF Thailand 2023](https://rsf.org/en/analyse_regionale/1013). Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand).

**Locator.** Sources’ descriptions of Move Forward’s first-place finish, Senate opposition, and formation of the subsequent coalition.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the inherited constitutional structure and moderate for the post-election parliamentary outcome. Prayut did not personally control every Senate vote, but the Senate was created and appointed under the military order he led.

**Contrary evidence.** The transfer from Prayut to Srettha occurred through parliamentary and constitutional procedures rather than a new coup.

**Cautions.** Senate voting motives were not uniform and should not be reduced to direct personal orders from Prayut.

### WEB-4B-03 — Election observers found meaningful competition but institutional weaknesses

**Claim.** ANFREL’s final observation characterized the 2023 election as showing accountability, transparency, and inclusiveness and reported that advance voting and Election Day were peaceful and orderly. It nevertheless issued recommendations intended to improve electoral democracy and institutional quality.

**Why it matters.** This provides a favorable counterweight to accounts that treat the election as wholly predetermined, while preserving evidence of structural weaknesses.

**Strongest source.** *2023 Thai General Election: Democracy at a Crossroads*, Asian Network for Free Elections, 15 August 2023: [ANFREL report announcement](https://anfrel.org/2023-thai-general-election-democracy-at-a-crossroads/).

**Locator.** Announcement’s summary of ANFREL’s final observation findings and recommendations.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The Election Commission and election administration were formally independent bodies, but the legal framework and institutional appointments were inherited from the military-backed order.

**Contrary evidence.** Independent observers were permitted to operate and the election produced an unexpected opposition victory.

**Cautions.** Full report details, including specific recommendations and polling-station findings, require direct PDF extraction.

### WEB-4B-04 — Military influence was constitutionally embedded

**Claim.** The 2017 constitution created a 250-seat Senate appointed by the military and gave the combined House and Senate a role in selecting the prime minister. This arrangement enabled Prayut to remain prime minister after the 2019 election and allowed military-aligned institutions to influence the 2023 succession.

**Why it matters.** The constitutional design was a mechanism of power entrenchment independent of any single election-day intervention.

**Strongest sources.** BTI 2024 Thailand report, pp. 3–10: [BTI report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf). Freedom House Thailand 2023: [Freedom in the World Thailand](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand/freedom-world/2023).

**Locator.** BTI discussion of the junta-created constitution, appointed Senate, and Prayut’s continuation in office; Freedom House sections on Senate appointment and prime-ministerial selection.

**Period fit.** Inherited institutional condition continuing into the target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong. Prayut led the junta that created the constitutional order and benefited from its design, though the constitution was formally enacted through institutions beyond his personal office.

**Contrary evidence.** The constitution also permitted competitive lower-house elections and did not prevent opposition parties from winning the largest bloc in 2023.

**Cautions.** The institutional system reflected military, monarchical, judicial, and bureaucratic power networks rather than Prayut alone.

### WEB-4B-05 — Political prosecution and pretrial detention

**Claim.** Authorities continued prosecuting activists, critics, and protesters in 2023. Human Rights Watch reported at least 258 people prosecuted during the year in relation to democracy protests or online comments under lèse-majesté charges. Thai Lawyers for Human Rights reported at least 67 political detainees during 2023, with restrictive bail and prolonged detention affecting many defendants.

**Why it matters.** Political freedom depends not only on voting but also on whether opponents can criticize and organize without criminal punishment or coercive detention.

**Strongest sources.** Human Rights Watch, *World Report 2024: Thailand*: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand). Thai Lawyers for Human Rights, *Overview of 2023 for political prisoners*: [TLHR report](https://tlhr2014.com/en/archives/64728).

**Locator.** HRW sections on expression, assembly, article 112, pretrial detention, and bail conditions; TLHR’s 2023 detention totals.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the continuation of the legal and prosecutorial environment under Prayut until August, though some cases continued under the successor government.

**Contrary evidence.** Courts sometimes granted bail, and opposition parties campaigned successfully in the election.

**Cautions.** TLHR is a legal-aid organization and HRW is a rights-monitoring organization; their counts should be compared with court records.

### WEB-4B-06 — Lèse-majesté and related laws constrained political expression

**Claim.** Thailand continued using Criminal Code section 112, sedition provisions, and the Computer-Related Crimes Act to prosecute political speech and online expression. Section 112 permitted prison sentences of up to 15 years per offense, and its use contributed to self-censorship and political exclusion.

**Why it matters.** The laws narrowed the range of permissible political criticism and made reform of the monarchy a high-risk electoral issue.

**Strongest sources.** Human Rights Watch: [HRW Thailand chapter](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/thailand). Freedom House: [Freedom in the World Thailand 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand/freedom-world/2023). Associated Press reporting on Arnon Nampa’s 2023 conviction: [AP report](https://apnews.com/article/7f794aac66dc2e1ae8f1933906595726).

**Locator.** HRW passages on 258 prosecutions and article 112; AP account of Arnon Nampa’s four-year sentence.

**Period fit.** Target period and inherited legal framework.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the government’s continued enforcement environment; individual prosecutions were conducted by police, prosecutors, and courts.

**Contrary evidence.** The law was publicly debated and Move Forward campaigned on reforming it, indicating that the issue could still enter electoral politics.

**Cautions.** Legal guilt and political criticism should be distinguished. The AP report documents a conviction, while broader claims about political motivation require legal and comparative analysis.

### WEB-4B-07 — Digital controls, surveillance, and election disinformation

**Claim.** Freedom House reported that Thailand’s internet environment remained “Not Free” in 2023. Ahead of the election, state-sponsored disinformation targeted opposition parties and figures; activists faced arrest, surveillance, and harassment; authorities used the Computer-Related Crimes Act for content removal and prosecutions; and the government retained significant influence over digital regulators and cybersecurity bodies.

**Why it matters.** Digital freedom was central to political organization and campaigning. State-sponsored disinformation and surveillance could distort competition even without shutting down the internet.

**Strongest source.** Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023: Thailand*: [Freedom on the Net Thailand](https://freedomhouse.org/country/thailand/freedom-net/2023).

**Locator.** Overview; sections on state-sponsored disinformation, content removal, surveillance, regulatory independence, and the Cybersecurity Act.

**Period fit.** Coverage period includes the 2023 election and target-year digital environment.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong to moderate. The regulatory structure and government bodies were created or shaped under the Prayut-led military order; individual disinformation campaigns require separate attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Internet access was broad, no major nationwide shutdown was reported during the coverage period, and social media remained a space for criticism.

**Cautions.** Freedom House uses a comparative methodology and draws on technical and local sources; the exact identity of state-sponsored disinformation actors may be disputed.

### WEB-4B-08 — Media ownership and regulatory pressure limited independence

**Claim.** Reporters Without Borders and the Reuters Institute reported that Thailand’s media environment remained constrained. Major outlets were concentrated among owners with links to the royal family, armed forces, or large business interests; defamation and cybercrime laws were used against journalists; and self-censorship concerning the monarchy was widespread.

**Why it matters.** Formal elections cannot provide full political choice if voters lack independent information and journalists cannot safely investigate those in power.

**Strongest sources.** Reporters Without Borders, *Thailand 2023*: [RSF Thailand 2023](https://rsf.org/en/analyse_regionale/1013). Reuters Institute, *Digital News Report 2023: Thailand*: [Reuters Institute Thailand](https://reutersinstitute.politics.ox.ac.uk/digital-news-report/2023/thailand).

**Locator.** RSF passages on media ownership, political influence, and the 2023 election; Reuters Institute passages on constrained critical reporting, defamation, cybercrime laws, and self-censorship.

**Period fit.** Target period and inherited media structure.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The government maintained the legal and regulatory system, but ownership concentration involved private, royal, military, and business actors.

**Contrary evidence.** Opposition and pro-reform media existed, and the election campaign allowed wider political debate than during direct junta rule.

**Cautions.** Ownership links and editorial influence can be difficult to prove causally. Outlet-by-outlet evidence is needed for stronger attribution.

### WEB-4B-09 — Courts and election institutions were active but politically contested

**Claim.** The Election Commission referred a case against Pita Limjaroenrat to the Constitutional Court, which suspended him from parliamentary duties in July 2023 while reviewing an alleged media-shareholding violation. Human Rights Watch described the suspension as a serious blow to democracy and politically motivated; Thai reporting presented the constitutional and electoral basis for the case.

**Why it matters.** The event illustrates the tension between formal institutional review and allegations that courts and election bodies were used to constrain the electoral winner.

**Strongest sources.** *The Nation Thailand*, “Constitutional Court accepts EC’s petition for review, suspends Pita as MP”: [court and EC report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/politics/40029496). Human Rights Watch, “Bogus Charges Keep Candidate from Top Post”: [HRW statement](https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/07/21/thailand-bogus-charges-keep-candidate-top-post).

**Locator.** The Nation account of the EC petition and seven-judge suspension decision; HRW assessment of the timing and political effect.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The case was formally initiated by the Election Commission and decided by the Constitutional Court, not directly ordered by Prayut. The institutions operated within a system created and staffed during the military-backed order.

**Contrary evidence.** Electoral laws applied through formal procedures, and the case was subject to judicial review rather than executive detention or military intervention.

**Cautions.** HRW’s “politically motivated” characterization is an evaluative conclusion. The full court record and Pita’s legal submissions should be consulted.

### WEB-4B-10 — Institutional capture and limited democratic resilience

**Claim.** BTI assessed Thailand’s 2023 political system as dominated by the monarchy and military, with a junta-appointed Senate and courts whose members had been indirectly selected through the post-coup order. It described the 2017 constitution and 2019 election as sustaining military dominance while acknowledging the existence of elected representatives and competitive opposition.

**Why it matters.** This is a comparative synthesis of the inherited authoritarian structure against which the 2023 election and transfer should be assessed.

**Strongest source.** Bertelsmann Stiftung, *BTI 2024 Country Report: Thailand*, published 2024, covering February 2021–January 2023: [BTI Thailand report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf).

**Locator.** Political participation, effective power to govern, freedom of association, expression, separation of powers, and judiciary sections, especially pp. 7–11.

**Period fit.** Primarily inherited-period context; only partly target-period.

**Ruler attribution.** Strong for the institutional inheritance from Prayut’s junta and government; weaker for events after January 2023.

**Contrary evidence.** Competitive parties, local elections, civil society, and the 2023 opposition victory provided real democratic openings.

**Cautions.** BTI combines empirical description with comparative judgments and does not cover the full 2023 election or transition.

### WEB-4B-11 — End-of-period democratic posture

**Claim.** Thailand ended 2023 with genuine electoral competition but without a normal democratic transfer from the military-backed order. The election enabled voters to reject Prayut’s party and elevate Move Forward, but the appointed Senate, contested courts, restrictive speech laws, political prosecutions, media constraints, and digital controls limited the practical effect of that electoral choice.

**Why it matters.** The period demonstrates partial democratic contestation within an authoritarian institutional framework rather than either full dictatorship or consolidated liberal democracy.

**Strongest sources.** ANFREL election observation; Freedom House; RSF; HRW; BTI.

**Locator.** Cross-source synthesis of WEB-4B-01 through WEB-4B-10.

**Period fit.** Target-period synthesis.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for the institutional framework and legal environment created or maintained under Prayut; moderate for specific post-election decisions by the Senate, courts, and successor coalition.

**Contrary evidence.** Prayut left office after the election, and the election was competitive enough to defeat the incumbent’s party.

**Cautions.** The later dissolution of Move Forward in 2024 is retrospective evidence and is not used as a target-year event here.

## 4. Disposition by lens

### 4B.1 — Genuine electoral choice

**Disposition: Partially evidenced.**

The election was competitive and voters could reject Prayut’s party. However, the appointed Senate and constitutional design prevented the largest opposition party from forming a government.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-04.

### 4B.2 — No entrenchment of power

**Disposition: Adverse evidence.**

The post-coup constitution, appointed Senate, and military-backed institutional design entrenched influence beyond ordinary electoral competition. Prayut did not remain prime minister after 2023, but the system constrained the consequences of the election.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-10.

### 4B.3 — Opposition and civic freedom

**Disposition: Adverse.**

Opposition parties could campaign and win seats, but activists, critics, protesters, and political lawyers faced criminal prosecution, detention, and restrictive bail.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-08.

### 4B.4 — Independent checks on power

**Disposition: Mixed and contested.**

Courts, the Election Commission, observers, and Parliament were active. Yet the appointment structure and political effects of institutional decisions generated serious concerns about independence and neutrality.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-09, WEB-4B-10.

### 4B.5 — Politically neutral institutions

**Disposition: Adverse or unresolved.**

The Senate and parts of the regulatory and judicial architecture were created or staffed through the military-backed order. Digital regulators and security-related bodies were heavily influenced by government officials.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-07, WEB-4B-10.

### 4B.6 — Independent information and media

**Disposition: Adverse.**

Media ownership concentration, self-censorship, criminal defamation, cybercrime prosecutions, and lèse-majesté restrictions limited independent political information.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-07, WEB-4B-08.

### 4B.7 — Equal political rights

**Disposition: Incomplete and uneven.**

The election included broad party competition, but unpopular viewpoints—especially criticism of the monarchy—faced unequal legal exposure. Children, activists, and regional or minority voices faced additional constraints.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-05, WEB-4B-06, WEB-4B-07.

### 4B.8 — Term limits and transfer

**Disposition: Mixed.**

Prayut did not remain in office after the 2023 transition, which is important evidence of constitutional transfer. But the Senate’s role and the obstruction of the largest party prevented a straightforward electoral succession.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-04, WEB-4B-09.

### 4B.9 — Digital freedom and surveillance

**Disposition: Adverse.**

Freedom House documented disinformation, surveillance, content removal, restrictive cyber laws, and weak regulatory independence. No nationwide internet shutdown was reported, and online criticism remained possible.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-07.

### 4B.10 — Lasting democratic resilience

**Disposition: Inconclusive, with limited positive movement.**

The 2023 election strengthened public opposition and demonstrated electoral contestability. It did not produce durable institutional liberalization because the military-backed constitutional order, restrictive laws, media pressures, and politicized checks remained in place.

Relevant records: WEB-4B-01, WEB-4B-02, WEB-4B-03, WEB-4B-11.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence concerns:

- election results;
- constitutional rules;
- Senate composition;
- court and Election Commission actions;
- prosecution and detention counts;
- internet restrictions and regulatory structures.

The weakest evidence concerns:

- private coordination between Prayut, military actors, Senate members, courts, and regulators;
- state-sponsored disinformation attribution;
- informal pressure on media owners;
- appointment decisions and loyalty networks;
- local government and provincial political rights;
- the practical effect of censorship on voters’ choices.

Official election and court records establish formal acts but do not by themselves establish neutrality. Rights organizations provide detailed case monitoring but may emphasize abuses. Election observers provide the strongest independent evidence on administration, though full ANFREL report extraction remained limited.

- [UN Special Procedures communication concerning the Election Commission and Move Forward](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TMResultsBase/DownLoadPublicCommunicationFile?gId=28985).

### Promising leads still needing inspection

- Full ANFREL interim and final PDF reports.
- Official Election Commission results and complaints records.
- Constitutional Court rulings on Pita and the election framework.
- UN Special Procedures communication concerning the Election Commission and Move Forward.
- RSF’s full country report and press-freedom ranking methodology.
- Official appointments and selection records for the Election Commission, Constitutional Court, NBTC, and Senate.
- Thai-language records concerning state-sponsored disinformation.
- Pegasus spyware evidence and government responses.
- Media ownership records and licensing or suspension decisions.
- Local-government and minority access to political participation.
- Full NIDA, Chulalongkorn, and Thai-language academic studies.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected or successfully opened: 24.
- Accepted evidence records: 11 underlying facts.
- Strongest evidence: election competitiveness, Senate obstruction, protest prosecution, digital controls, and media constraints.
- Main unresolved questions: exact observer findings, appointment independence, state-sponsored disinformation attribution, media ownership influence, and the degree of Prayut’s personal direction over institutional decisions.
- No score assigned.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

# Chapter 5B — Economic Well-Being and Prosperity

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha governed Thailand until August 2023. The economy was recovering from the COVID-19 shock, with tourism and private consumption returning, but the recovery remained slow relative to regional peers and highly exposed to external demand and tourism.

Thailand’s 2023 performance had several positive features:

- inflation declined sharply from 2022;
- tourism and private consumption recovered;
- unemployment remained relatively low;
- the current account moved toward surplus; and
- public finances remained broadly manageable.

But the underlying record was weaker:

- real GDP growth was only about 2.5%;
- growth remained below Thailand’s longer-term potential and slower than many ASEAN peers;
- household and public debt were high;
- private and public investment remained subdued;
- inequality, regional concentration, weak skills, and stagnant labor incomes persisted;
- the economy remained dependent on tourism and vulnerable to China and external demand; and
- competition and investment rules continued to favor large or politically connected firms in several sectors.

Prayut’s direct attribution is strongest for macroeconomic management, fiscal policy, energy subsidies, investment-climate regulation, and inherited institutional conditions. It is weaker for final-year economic outcomes because the government changed in August and tourism, global demand, exchange rates, and post-pandemic reopening were major external influences.

| WEB-5B-03 | Household debt and weak investment remained major structural constraints | Target period and inherited condition | 5B.3, 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-04 | IMF recommended structural reform, better social protection, progressive taxation, and productivity investment | Target period retrospective | 5B.1, 5B.3, 5B.6, 5B.7, 5B.8 |
| WEB-5B-05 | Inequality was driven by skills, education, regional, gender, agricultural, and household-debt gaps | Target period and inherited condition | 5B.1, 5B.6, 5B.8, 5B.10 |
| WEB-5B-06 | The investment and competition environment contained exemptions and advantages for state-owned or connected interests | Target-period policy environment | 5B.4, 5B.5 |

## 3. Developed evidence records

### WEB-5B-01 — Modest post-pandemic recovery

**Claim.** Thailand’s economy continued recovering in 2023, supported by private consumption and tourism, but growth remained modest. The IMF reported that activity expanded by 1.9% in the first three quarters and projected full-year real GDP growth of 2.5%. The World Bank had earlier projected 3.9%, indicating that the recovery underperformed earlier expectations.

**Why it matters.** The economy improved, but the result was not a strong broad-based acceleration.

**Strongest sources.** IMF, *Thailand: 2023 Article IV Consultation*, published January 2024: [IMF report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/files/publications/cr/2024/english/1thaea2024001.pdf). World Bank, *Thailand Economic Monitor*, June 2023: [World Bank update](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/06/28/thai-economy-to-accelerate-in-2023-as-demand-from-major-economies-picks-up-tourists-return).

**Locator.** IMF pp. 1–3: 2023 growth, tourism, private consumption, weak external demand, and investment. World Bank summary: projected acceleration from 2.6% in 2022 to 3.9% in 2023.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut governed until August and shaped fiscal, tourism, and regulatory policy, but reopening, foreign tourism, global demand, and monetary policy were major independent causes.

**Contrary evidence.** Tourism recovery, stronger consumption, and low inflation were genuine positive outcomes.

**Cautions.** The World Bank figure is a forecast, not the final outcome. IMF figures are retrospective but incorporate data available after the government transition.

### WEB-5B-02 — Inflation fell, but fiscal space narrowed

**Claim.** Inflation declined from 6.1% in 2022 to an IMF-projected 1.3% average in 2023, helped by energy-price measures, base effects, and monetary tightening. At the same time, public-sector debt was projected at 62.4% of GDP, and the IMF warned that fiscal discipline was needed to preserve macroeconomic stability.

**Why it matters.** The government helped manage the cost-of-living shock, but the response operated within tighter fiscal constraints.

**Strongest source.** IMF 2023 Article IV report: [IMF report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/files/publications/cr/2024/english/1thaea2024001.pdf).

**Locator.** IMF pp. 1–3: inflation, energy subsidies, fiscal balance, and public debt.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate to strong for energy subsidies and fiscal choices; monetary policy was primarily the responsibility of the Bank of Thailand, whose operational independence limited direct prime-ministerial attribution.

**Contrary evidence.** Inflation remained within the authorities’ target range and macroeconomic stability was maintained.

**Cautions.** Lower inflation partly reflected base effects and global price changes, not solely government competence.

### WEB-5B-03 — Debt and weak investment constrained prosperity

**Claim.** Thailand entered and completed 2023 with elevated household and public debt and weak investment. The IMF identified elevated private-sector debt as a financial-stability risk and reported gross fixed investment growth of only 2.0% in 2023, with gross domestic investment declining as a share of GDP.

**Why it matters.** Debt and weak investment limit household resilience, productivity, and the government’s ability to respond to future shocks.

**Strongest source.** IMF 2023 Article IV report: [IMF report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/files/publications/cr/2024/english/1thaea2024001.pdf).

**Locator.** IMF pp. 1–3 and Table 1: private debt risk, gross fixed investment, public debt, and fiscal space.

**Period fit.** Target period and inherited structural condition.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The problem reflected long-term household borrowing, pandemic support, financial-sector conditions, and structural policy choices across governments.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand maintained financial stability and the IMF welcomed the gradual normalization of financial-sector interventions.

**Cautions.** Household debt statistics vary by definition and institution. The government’s exact contribution to debt accumulation requires fiscal and regulatory time series.

### WEB-5B-04 — IMF called for structural reform and more targeted distribution

**Claim.** The IMF concluded that Thailand needed productivity-enhancing structural reforms, greater investment in human and physical capital, stronger social safety nets, more progressive taxation, improved labor skills, and reforms to state-owned enterprises and extra-budgetary funds.

**Why it matters.** These recommendations identify the gap between macroeconomic stability and broad, durable prosperity.

**Strongest source.** IMF Executive Board assessment, January 2024, based on the 2023 consultation: [IMF Article IV report](https://www.imf.org/en/news/articles/2024/01/22/pr2420-tha-imf-executive-board-concludes-2023-article-iv-consultation-with-thailand).

**Locator.** Passages recommending gradual normalization, targeted support for vulnerable groups, progressive taxation, debt reduction, investment, productivity reform, and social-protection reform.

**Period fit.** Retrospective assessment of the target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The recommendations concern policies under both the Prayut and Srettha governments; they diagnose inherited weaknesses more than a single cabinet’s failure.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand had already maintained macroeconomic stability and social programs that mitigated the pandemic shock.

**Cautions.** IMF recommendations are expert policy judgments, not direct evidence that each recommended measure was feasible or politically available to Prayut.

### WEB-5B-05 — Inequality and opportunity gaps persisted

**Claim.** The World Bank reported that Thailand’s relatively high inequality reflected gaps in worker skills, education quality, female labor-force participation, agricultural productivity, regional opportunity, and household debt. The report emphasized that the pandemic may have worsened learning outcomes and debt challenges.

**Why it matters.** Aggregate GDP and tourism recovery did not ensure equal gains across households or regions.

**Strongest source.** World Bank, “Focus on Skills, Education, Regional Development Needed to Create a More Equal Society in Thailand,” 29 November 2023: [World Bank inequality report](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/11/29/focus-on-skills-education-regional-development-needed-to-create-a-more-equal-society-in-thailand).

**Locator.** Press-release passages on skills, digital education, female labor participation, agricultural productivity, learning loss, and household debt.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. These were long-term structural conditions, but education, labor, regional, and agricultural policies were within government responsibility.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand had broad public-service access and prior poverty reduction, and the post-pandemic recovery improved employment and consumption.

**Cautions.** The source presents national-level structural analysis and does not identify which 2023 programs would have changed the trends.

### WEB-5B-06 — Competition rules left important exemptions

**Claim.** The U.S. State Department’s 2023 Investment Climate Statement reported that Thailand’s Trade Competition Act exempted state-owned enterprises and several activities involving national security, public benefit, public utilities, government agencies, and other legally exempted enterprises.

**Why it matters.** Broad exemptions can protect public-interest functions but can also weaken competitive neutrality and preserve advantages for state-linked or politically connected firms.

**Strongest source.** U.S. Department of State, *2023 Investment Climate Statements: Thailand*: [Thailand investment-climate statement](https://2021-2025.state.gov/reports/2023-investment-climate-statements/thailand/).

**Locator.** Competition-policy section describing the Trade Competition Act and exemptions.

**Period fit.** Target-period investment environment.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. The statutory framework predated 2023, but Prayut’s cabinet administered the investment and competition regime.

**Contrary evidence.** Exemptions may be legally intended for public utilities, security, or public-interest services rather than favoritism. The source does not prove a specific corrupt award or monopoly.

**Cautions.** This is an investment-climate assessment prepared by a foreign government and aimed partly at foreign investors. Specific enforcement cases and competition-authority decisions require separate inspection.

### WEB-5B-07 — Professional economic institutions operated with relative independence

**Claim.** The IMF reported that Thailand’s monetary policy gradually normalized and judged the Bank of Thailand’s neutral monetary stance appropriate. The report also described fiscal and monetary authorities’ efforts to preserve stability amid shocks.

**Why it matters.** Economic leadership was not wholly personalized: professional institutions retained operational roles, particularly in monetary policy and financial stability.

**Strongest source.** IMF 2023 Article IV report: [IMF report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/files/publications/cr/2024/english/1thaea2024001.pdf).

**Locator.** IMF Executive Board assessment on monetary policy, exchange rates, financial-sector normalization, and fiscal policy.

**Period fit.** Target period.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut’s government set the legal and political environment, but the Bank of Thailand had operational autonomy.

**Contrary evidence.** The IMF also warned about limited fiscal discipline and government policy risks, indicating that institutional competence did not eliminate political pressure or macroeconomic uncertainty.

**Cautions.** Personnel appointments and informal political influence over economic officials were not fully investigated.

### WEB-5B-08 — Labor recovery coexisted with informality and unequal conditions

**Claim.** Thailand’s labor market recovered alongside tourism and domestic demand, but informal employment remained structurally large and migrant workers faced weaker protections. BTI reported that informal employment represented about 65% of the workforce in 2022. ILO materials identified low pay and difficult conditions for migrant agricultural workers.

**Why it matters.** Employment levels alone do not show whether prosperity was fairly distributed or whether workers had predictable, decent conditions.

**Strongest sources.** Bertelsmann Stiftung, BTI 2024 Thailand report: [BTI report](https://bti-project.org/fileadmin/api/content/en/downloads/reports/country_report_2024_THA.pdf). ILO, “Labour Act Exclusion Leads to Low Pay and Challenging Conditions for Migrant Workers in Thai Agriculture”: [ILO report](https://www.ilo.org/resource/news/labour-act-exclusion-leads-low-pay-and-challenging-conditions-migrant).

**Locator.** BTI economic-performance section on informal employment; ILO discussion of labor-law exclusion, low pay, and migrant-worker conditions.

**Period fit.** Inherited labor structure with target-period relevance.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut’s government administered labor law and migrant-worker policy, but the informal economy and labor-market structure were longstanding.

**Contrary evidence.** Employment and wages improved as tourism and consumption recovered, and Thailand maintained substantial industrial and service employment.

**Cautions.** The BTI figure is from 2022, not 2023. ILO reporting on migrant agriculture cannot be generalized automatically to all workers.

### WEB-5B-09 — Investment and productivity remained below long-term needs

**Claim.** IMF analysis identified weak domestic investment, low potential growth, declining competitiveness, skills gaps, and the need for more investment in human and physical capital. The World Bank similarly emphasized productivity, education, and regional-development reform.

**Why it matters.** Short-term recovery did not clearly establish a stronger long-term trajectory.

**Strongest sources.** IMF 2023 Article IV report: [IMF report](https://www.imf.org/-/media/files/publications/cr/2024/english/1thaea2024001.pdf). World Bank inequality and opportunity report: [World Bank report](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/11/29/focus-on-skills-education-regional-development-needed-to-create-a-more-equal-society-in-thailand).

**Locator.** IMF recommendations on productivity, competitiveness, skills, investment, and climate resilience; World Bank recommendations on skills, education, and agriculture.

**Period fit.** Target period and inherited structural condition.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. These constraints reflected years of policy and external shocks, but Prayut’s government did not demonstrate a decisive correction before leaving office.

**Contrary evidence.** Thailand maintained infrastructure, a diversified manufacturing base, and growing tourism, and it attracted some foreign investment.

**Cautions.** Long-term productivity effects cannot be judged from one year. Project-level audits and execution data are needed.

### WEB-5B-10 — End-of-period economic trajectory

**Claim.** Thailand ended 2023 with macroeconomic stability and post-pandemic recovery but not a clearly stronger or fairer economic trajectory. Growth was slow, investment weak, household and public debt high, inequality persistent, and dependence on tourism and external demand significant.

**Why it matters.** This is the appropriate period-level conclusion without reducing prosperity to GDP growth.

**Strongest sources.** IMF, World Bank, Bank of Thailand, World Bank inequality analysis, ILO, and the U.S. State Department investment-climate statement.

**Locator.** Cross-source synthesis of WEB-5B-01 through WEB-5B-09.

**Period fit.** Target-period synthesis.

**Ruler attribution.** Moderate. Prayut’s cabinet influenced fiscal, labor, investment, and tourism policy until August; post-pandemic reopening and global conditions were major independent factors.

**Contrary evidence.** Inflation declined, tourism recovered, employment improved, and macroeconomic stability was preserved.

**Cautions.** Some final 2023 outcomes and subsequent policy decisions belong to the Srettha government and should not be attributed to Prayut.

## 4. Disposition by lens

### 5B.1 — Broad and sustainable prosperity

**Disposition: Mixed, with limited evidence of transformation.**

The government supported recovery and macroeconomic stability, but structural reform, productivity, distribution, and regional opportunity remained unresolved.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-04, WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-09, WEB-5B-10.

### 5B.2 — Qualified economic leadership

**Disposition: Partially evidenced.**

The Bank of Thailand and planning institutions retained professional roles. Evidence is insufficient to determine whether economic appointments were consistently merit-based or whether political loyalty shaped senior economic personnel.

Relevant record: WEB-5B-07.

### 5B.3 — Macroeconomic stability

**Disposition: Generally positive but constrained.**

Inflation declined and financial stability was maintained. Public and private debt, limited fiscal space, and dependence on tourism remained significant vulnerabilities.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-02, WEB-5B-03, WEB-5B-07.

### 5B.4 — Fair economic rules

**Disposition: Mixed and incomplete.**

Thailand had a formal competition law and investment framework, but exemptions for state-owned and public-interest entities weakened competitive neutrality. Labor informality and migrant-worker exclusions also reduced equal economic protection.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-06, WEB-5B-08.

### 5B.5 — Resistance to economic capture

**Disposition: Inconclusive, with material concerns.**

The investment-climate record identifies exemptions and governance risks, while earlier reconnaissance identified corruption controversies. Direct 2023 procurement, monopoly, and enforcement records were not sufficiently inspected to establish a specific capture case attributable to Prayut.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-06 and the investment-climate source.

### 5B.6 — Productive public investment

**Disposition: Incomplete.**

The IMF and World Bank identified continuing needs for human capital, infrastructure, skills, technology, and regional investment. The available sources do not yet establish whether 2023 appropriations were executed effectively or produced measurable productivity gains.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-04, WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-09.

### 5B.7 — Evidence and correction

**Disposition: Mixed.**

Economic data were published by professional institutions and the government accepted external scrutiny. However, the available evidence does not show a major policy correction during Prayut’s final months in response to weak growth, debt, inequality, or investment stagnation.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-04, WEB-5B-07, WEB-5B-10.

### 5B.8 — Fair distribution

**Disposition: Adverse or unresolved.**

Inequality, regional opportunity gaps, informal employment, migrant-worker vulnerability, and household debt remained substantial. Recovery benefits were not demonstrably shared evenly.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-03, WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-08.

### 5B.9 — Managing economic shocks

**Disposition: Mixed.**

The government helped manage inflation and supported reopening and tourism. However, debt exposure, energy subsidies, weak investment, and dependence on external demand left limited room for future shocks.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-01, WEB-5B-02, WEB-5B-03.

### 5B.10 — Lasting economic trajectory

**Disposition: Inconclusive, with evidence of stability but not decisive improvement.**

Thailand left 2023 with recovery and macroeconomic stability but remained slow-growing, unequal, debt-constrained, and dependent on tourism. The evidence does not establish that Prayut left a stronger and fairer long-term trajectory than the one he inherited.

Relevant records: WEB-5B-04, WEB-5B-05, WEB-5B-09, WEB-5B-10.

## 5. Information environment and limitations

Reliable information was strongest for:

- GDP, inflation, fiscal balance, debt, and investment;
- IMF and World Bank macroeconomic assessments;
- monetary-policy direction;
- formal competition and investment rules; and
- broad inequality and labor-market conditions.

It was weaker for:

- actual execution of development and infrastructure budgets;
- household median income and consumption in 2023;
- distribution of tourism gains;
- regional and gender-specific labor outcomes;
- politically connected procurement;
- state-owned-enterprise privileges;
- tax incidence;
- informal employment and migrant-worker earnings; and
- whether Prayut personally directed economic decisions later attributed to the cabinet.

Current-price GDP and GDP per capita should not be treated as real household prosperity. Tourism recovery may improve national output while leaving debt, wages, and regional inequality unresolved. Forecasts from mid-2023 also differ materially from final estimates.

### Promising leads still needing inspection

- Final NESDC 2023 national-account and household-income releases.
- Bank of Thailand 2023 annual-report PDF and monetary-policy minutes.
- Fiscal-year 2023 budget execution, procurement, and infrastructure-completion data.
- State Audit Office findings on major economic and infrastructure projects.
- Competition Commission decisions involving state-owned enterprises and large conglomerates.
- Thailand’s 2023 household socioeconomic survey.
- Regional, gender, migrant, and informal-worker income data.
- Tax incidence and social-protection coverage.
- Tourism employment, wages, and regional distribution.
- World Bank’s full *Bridging the Gap: Inequality and Jobs in Thailand* report.
- ILO labor-force and decent-work outcome tables.

## 7. Source counts and remaining gaps

- Candidate sources inspected or successfully opened: 28.
- Accepted evidence records: 10 underlying facts.
- Strongest evidence: macroeconomic stability, modest recovery, debt risks, inequality, and structural reform needs.
- Main unresolved questions: budget execution, actual household income and consumption, distribution of tourism gains, competition enforcement, state-owned-enterprise privileges, and Prayut-specific economic appointments.
- No score assigned.
