--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

# 6B — Social Well-Being and Human Development, Thailand, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was prime minister until August 2023. He controlled the cabinet and national policy agenda but operated through a coalition, a powerful bureaucracy, and institutions shaped by military rule. Social outcomes therefore reflect both his government’s choices and inherited systems, especially universal health coverage, local-government delivery, the education system, and pandemic recovery.

The evidence shows substantial administrative and health-service capacity alongside persistent distributional weaknesses. Thailand’s universal health system covered nearly all citizens and NHSO reported extensive 2023 service delivery. However, independent assessments found low social-assistance adequacy, insufficient education and elderly-care coordination, persistent regional and socioeconomic education gaps, and barriers for migrant and domestic workers. Many outcomes cannot be attributed solely to Prayut because implementation was shared with ministries, local authorities, NHSO, schools, and statutory agencies.

## 2. Evidence index

### WEB-6B-01 — Universal health coverage and 2023 service delivery

Thailand’s NHSO reported that in fiscal year 2023 the Universal Coverage Scheme covered more than 47.2 million people through 17,247 public and private health units, including outpatient, inpatient, chronic-disease, mental-health, HIV, kidney-care, preventive, and primary-care services. It also expanded after-hours clinics, added 13 benefits, and operated patient complaints and referral systems.

This is strong evidence of durable service capacity and formal welfare administration. It is an official self-report, so utilization and coverage claims should be checked against independent health-system data. Prayut attribution is moderate: the scheme is statutory and administered by NHSO, but national budgets and cabinet policy supported it during his tenure.

Source: *Top 10 achievements of the NHSO in 2023*, National Health Security Office, 26 March 2024, especially paragraphs describing coverage, services, benefits, and complaints. [NHSO report](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US)

Lenses: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.4, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-02 — Thailand maintained a strong inherited universal-health platform

The World Bank reported that approximately 98 percent of Thailand’s population was covered by one of three public health-insurance schemes in 2021, with a service-coverage index of 82 percent and approximately 2 percent of people spending more than 10 percent of household expenditure on health care. The report described Thailand’s health system as resilient during COVID-19.

This is mainly inherited and contextual evidence, not a 2023 outcome. It establishes a substantial institutional baseline against which Prayut-era continuity and later improvements should be judged. The World Bank is authoritative, but the most detailed figures predate 2023.

Source: *Thailand Public Revenue and Spending Assessment: Promoting an Inclusive and Sustainable Future*, World Bank, June 2023, Chapter 4, pp. 73 onward. [World Bank assessment](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099052523201027923/pdf/P1771570af8b360b40aab305f0be65a2678.pdf)

Lenses: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-03 — Social spending and elderly benefits were inadequate for future needs

The World Bank’s May 2023 assessment concluded that Thailand faced rising costs from aging, pensions, healthcare, education, and climate adaptation. It specifically found that the Old Age Allowance and other social-assistance payments were low by international standards, while per-student spending at pre-primary and secondary levels lagged international benchmarks. It recommended more efficient social-assistance targeting, higher benefit adequacy, stronger education spending, preventive healthcare, and progressive revenue reform.

This is important evidence that formal social commitments were not adequately funded relative to identified needs. It is an independent diagnostic rather than a direct audit of Prayut’s personal decisions. The report was published during his term, but many recommendations concern long-term structural conditions.

Source: *Increased Revenue Required to Meet Thailand’s Public Spending Needs*, World Bank, 29 May 2023, paragraphs 123–132. [World Bank press release](https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/05/29/increased-revenue-required-to-meet-thailand-s-public-spending-needs)

Lenses: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-04 — Education access remained unequal, especially at secondary level

UNICEF reported that primary enrollment was almost universal, but lower- and upper-secondary enrollment was substantially lower, at approximately 86 percent and 69 percent respectively. Disadvantaged communities, migrants, and children with disabilities were disproportionately represented among children out of school. Learning outcomes were associated with socioeconomic status, rural school resources, and whether Thai was spoken at home. UNICEF also reported extensive learning loss after COVID-19 school closures and continuing challenges involving internet access, school safety, mental health, and dropout.

This shows broad access but unequal effective opportunity and quality. UNICEF is a strong international source, though its page synthesizes multiple datasets and does not isolate decisions made by Prayut’s cabinet in 2023.

Source: *Education*, UNICEF Thailand, publication date not stated, current country-programme page. [UNICEF Thailand education assessment](https://www.unicef.org/thailand/what-we-do/education)

Lenses: 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.5, 6B.6, 6B.8, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-05 — Vulnerable children experienced continuing learning loss

The Equitable Education Fund’s 2023 Educational Inequality Report identified learning loss among children in vulnerable communities and linked educational disadvantage to the incomplete economic recovery from COVID-19. EEF’s related program material described financial barriers, unequal access to learning technology, household-income pressures, and the need for targeted meals, internet access, and localized education models.

EEF is a specialized Thai institution focused on educational inequality and provides an important local perspective. Its program material also advocates for its policy approach, so its findings should be cross-checked against official education statistics and UNICEF assessments. The evidence concerns 2023 conditions but reflects pandemic-era inheritance.

Source: *According to the 2023 Educational Inequality Report, children in vulnerable communities are experiencing learning loss*, Equitable Education Fund, 25 October 2023. [EEF report](https://research.eef.or.th/children-in-vulnerable-communities-are-experiencing-learning-loss/)

Lenses: 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.5, 6B.6, 6B.8, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-06 — Elderly policy implementation was fragmented and weakly coordinated

Thailand’s State Audit Office later reported that implementation of the National Plan for the Elderly lacked coordination among agencies and local authorities. Many organizations operated in silos; only a limited number of provinces had specific action plans; local officials lacked adequate awareness and training; and updates to the plan were delayed. The audit recommended a central coordinating body, measurable provincial plans, training, and accelerated updating.

This is later retrospective evidence about a plan operating during and beyond Prayut’s tenure. It is highly relevant to durable institutions, vulnerable-group protection, and measurement, but it does not establish that Prayut personally ordered the deficiencies. The audit’s own publication is an official institutional account and should be supplemented with the underlying audit file and local implementation evidence.

Source: *Case Study: Inclusive Audit of Thailand’s National Plan for the Elderly by the State Audit Office of Thailand*, State Audit Office of Thailand, 16 August 2024, paragraphs 88–121. [State Audit Office case study](https://www.audit.go.th/eng/case-study-inclusive-audit-thailands-national-plan-elderly-state-audit-office-sao-thailand)

Lenses: 6B.3, 6B.4, 6B.5, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-07 — Domestic workers faced limited effective social protection

An ILO report published in 2023 examined domestic workers’ access to social protection in Thailand and identified practical barriers affecting access to care and social-protection programs. The report is directly relevant to workers whose employment falls outside standard formal-sector arrangements.

This supports a finding of unequal access despite broad national welfare institutions. It does not by itself show whether the barriers resulted from a Prayut decision, administrative neglect, labor-law design inherited from earlier governments, or employer practices. The report is short and should be read with its underlying data and methodology.

Source: Jenna Holliday, *Protection in practice: challenges and perceptions of domestic workers accessing social protection in Thailand*, International Labour Organization, 2023. [ILO report record](https://researchrepository.ilo.org/esploro/outputs/report/Protection-in-practice-challenges-and-perceptions/995264827402676)

Lenses: 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.7, 6B.8, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-08 — Migrants and noncitizens faced weaker access to social protection

The candidate IOM report concerns social protection for migrant workers and their families in Thailand. Its subject is materially important because Thailand’s national systems are broad but frequently organized around nationality, registration, employment status, and formal documentation. The source was not retrievable in full because the IOM PDF returned a 403 access error; it remains a corroboration lead rather than fully extracted evidence.

The 2023 U.S. State Department human-rights report independently documented poor and overcrowded immigration detention conditions, limited medical access, and restrictions affecting undocumented migrants, refugees, and asylum seekers. That report is primarily a rights source rather than a welfare evaluation, but it confirms that noncitizen access was uneven.

Source: *Social Protection for Migrant Workers and Their Families in Thailand*, International Organization for Migration, publication date not confirmed. [IOM report](https://publications.iom.int/system/files/pdf/Social-Protection-for-Migrant-Workers-in-Thailand.pdf); corroboration: *2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Thailand*, U.S. Department of State, 2024, sections on detention and migrants. [U.S. State Department report](https://www.ecoi.net/de/dokument/2107649.html)

Lenses: 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.7, 6B.8, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-09 — Fiscal policy helped protect households during COVID-19, but social spending remained low

The World Bank reported that Thailand’s COVID-19 social-assistance response offset income loss and reduced the increase in poverty and inequality. It also concluded that social spending and tax collection remained low by international standards and recommended better targeting, expanded human-development spending, and progressive revenue increases.

This is favorable evidence of crisis-response capacity but adverse evidence about the sustainability and adequacy of the social-protection model. The central estimates concern 2021 and fiscal conditions around 2022–23, so they are inherited or contextual rather than a clean measure of 2023 outcomes. Prayut attribution is moderate because the response occurred largely under his government, while the longer-term fiscal structure was not his alone.

Source: *Thailand Economic Monitor: Fiscal Policy for a Resilient and Equitable Future*, World Bank, December 2022, key findings. [World Bank Economic Monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/thailand/publication/thailand-economic-monitor-fiscal-policy-for-a-resilient-and-equitable-future)

Lenses: 6B.1, 6B.2, 6B.3, 6B.6, 6B.9, 6B.10.

### WEB-6B-10 — Health administration showed monitoring and complaint mechanisms

NHSO reported that it developed a five-year 2023–27 patient-rights action plan, addressed extra billing, upgraded its contact center, coordinated referrals, and used monitoring and evaluation for new health innovations. It also reported near-universal participation by local administrative organizations in local health funds.

This is evidence of professional administration, local institutional integration, and formal correction mechanisms. It is based on NHSO’s own account and does not independently verify whether complaint resolution was effective or whether all regions received equal service quality.

Source: *Top 10 achievements of the NHSO in 2023*, NHSO, 26 March 2024, paragraphs 63–75. [NHSO report](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US)

Lenses: 6B.4, 6B.5, 6B.7, 6B.9.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **6B.1 — Welfare as a governing purpose:** Supported but qualified. Universal health coverage and pandemic assistance show real welfare commitments; World Bank evidence indicates that benefit adequacy and social spending remained insufficient. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 02, 03, 09.
- **6B.2 — Access to essential services:** Mixed. Health access was broad; education and social-protection access remained unequal. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 04, 07, 08.
- **6B.3 — Priority for vulnerable groups:** Incomplete and uneven. Targeted health and education programs existed, but migrants, disadvantaged children, domestic workers, and older people faced gaps. Evidence: WEB-6B-03–08.
- **6B.4 — Professional service delivery:** Mixed-positive in health administration, weaker in elderly-plan coordination and education equality. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 06, 10.
- **6B.5 — Measurement and correction:** Partial. NHSO used monitoring, complaints, and action plans; the elderly audit found weak coordination, delayed updates, and inadequate measurement. Evidence: WEB-6B-06, 10.
- **6B.6 — Protection during crises:** The COVID response reduced hardship, but learning loss and fiscal constraints persisted. Evidence: WEB-6B-04, 05, 09.
- **6B.7 — No political allocation of welfare:** No strong evidence establishes systematic partisan allocation of ordinary welfare. Formal systems were broad, but nationality, registration, and employment status created major exclusions. This lens remains partly unresolved.
- **6B.8 — Dignity and equal opportunity:** Unequal education outcomes, migrant barriers, and domestic-worker exclusion weigh against equal opportunity; health coverage is a countervailing strength. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 04, 07, 08.
- **6B.9 — Durable social institutions:** Strongest evidence concerns NHSO and the three-scheme health-insurance structure. Elderly-care coordination and social-assistance adequacy were weaker. Evidence: WEB-6B-01, 02, 03, 06.
- **6B.10 — Lasting life chances:** Cannot be established conclusively from 2023 alone. Health institutions remained strong, but education recovery, inequality, vulnerable-group access, and aging policy remained unresolved. Evidence: WEB-6B-03–09.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Reliable information was available from the World Bank, UNICEF, ILO, NHSO, and the State Audit Office, but sources differ substantially in independence and time fit. Official NHSO material is strong for reported activities and coverage but weaker for independent outcome verification. International assessments are stronger for comparison and diagnosis but often use data from earlier years. The State Audit Office’s elderly-care findings are retrospective and do not isolate Prayut’s personal responsibility. Migrant and domestic-worker evidence is thinner than national health data, and one IOM source could not be fully opened because of access restrictions.

National averages also conceal regional, urban-rural, nationality, disability, and socioeconomic differences. The 2023 transition of prime ministers creates an attribution problem: Prayut governed roughly the first eight months, while some annual reports describe the full fiscal year or later retrospective outcomes. No evidence inspected demonstrates that welfare was systematically allocated according to party loyalty, but the available material is insufficient to rule out localized political favoritism.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully extracted evidence

World Bank public-spending assessment; World Bank Economic Monitor; NHSO 2023 achievements; UNICEF Thailand education page; State Audit Office elderly-plan case study; ILO domestic-worker report record; U.S. State Department human-rights report; EEF 2023 educational-inequality report.

### Opened mainly for corroboration or context

World Bank inequality and jobs material; World Bank country page; ADB Thailand country page; Department of Social Development and Welfare Annual Report 2023; NHSO annual-report portal; State Audit Office audit archive; UNICEF Thailand reports portal; IOM Migration Governance Indicators profile; academic elderly-welfare articles; ILO Thailand country materials; AP Thailand archive; BTI 2024 Thailand report.

### Promising leads still requiring fuller inspection

- IOM, *Social Protection for Migrant Workers and Their Families in Thailand* — PDF access returned 403.
- Department of Social Development and Welfare, *Annual Report 2023* — PDF endpoint returned an access/fetch error.
- World Bank *Bridging the Gap: Inequality and Jobs in Thailand* — requires extraction of regional and labor-market findings.
- FAO/AP food-insecurity report — candidate not fully opened.
- Underlying State Audit Office report and provincial elderly-plan evidence.
- National education budget execution and learning-outcome data for 2023.
- Thailand’s 2023 household and social-protection administrative microdata.

### Rejected or not retained

General academic catalogues, repositories, and broad political sources that did not provide a specific, independently usable 2023 social-welfare fact were not retained as evidence.

## 6. Counts and remaining gaps

- Fully extracted evidence records: 10.
- Candidate sources inspected or opened: more than 12.
- Main source families: World Bank, UNICEF, NHSO, State Audit Office, ILO, EEF, U.S. State Department, IOM.
- Principal gaps: executed social-protection spending, benefit uptake by region and group, 2023 education outcomes, food insecurity, housing and WASH outcomes, migrant coverage rates, and direct evidence connecting cabinet decisions to observed social changes.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

# 7B — Ruler Personal Integrity and Honesty, Thailand, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was prime minister until September 2023, having governed most of the year and contested the May election. His personal integrity must be distinguished from corruption in the wider military, police, procurement, and coalition system. The evidence reviewed contains no final finding that Prayut personally accepted bribes, concealed assets, or improperly profited from office in 2023.

The record is nevertheless mixed. Prayut made public anti-corruption commitments and ordered investigations into police corruption, while independent reporting and official proceedings documented persistent corruption risks, weak accountability, opaque networks, and conflicts involving senior political allies. His post-office asset declaration showed a modest increase in declared household assets, but this is evidence of disclosure, not proof of honesty or innocence beyond the declared information. The strongest adverse evidence concerns institutional tolerance and weak accountability rather than a proven personal enrichment scheme.

## 2. Evidence index

### WEB-7B-01 — Prayut’s declared household assets increased modestly during his tenure

After leaving office, Prayut declared personal assets of 98.66 million baht and household assets with his spouse of 130.19 million baht, with no reported liabilities. The reported combined assets were 2.18 million baht higher than the household declaration made when he became prime minister in 2014.

This is relevant to disclosure, personal enrichment, and public trust. It does not establish that the declarations were complete or independently verified, nor does it show that the increase came from corruption. The source is reputable reporting on NACC-disclosed declarations, but the underlying NACC filing should be inspected directly.

Source: *The net worth of Prayut, Prawit increased by around THB2m during 9 years in power*, The Nation Thailand, 24 November 2023, paragraphs 9–32. [The Nation report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/general/40033180)

Lenses: 7B.1, 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.6, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-02 — The Constitutional Court rejected a conflict-of-interest challenge concerning military housing

The Constitutional Court ruled that Prayut’s continued use of military housing did not constitute a prohibited conflict of interest or improper special benefit and did not violate the constitutional ethical standard for ministers. The ruling predates 2023 but remained part of the legal and political baseline during the requested year.

This is formal exculpatory evidence, but it was controversial because the court’s interpretation was contested by legal scholars and political opponents. It establishes the legal outcome, not that the arrangement was ethically unproblematic in public perception.

Source: *Constitutional Court Ruling No. 5/2563*, Constitutional Court of Thailand, 2 December 2020. [Official court ruling](https://constitutionalcourt.or.th/occ_en/download/article/article_20221213132753.pdf); legal analysis: [Thai Legal Studies analysis](https://so01.tci-thaijo.org/index.php/tls/article/download/263851/173309)

Lenses: 7B.2, 7B.3, 7B.4, 7B.6, 7B.7.

### WEB-7B-03 — Prayut publicly ordered investigation of alleged police gambling corruption

In February 2023, Prayut said police were investigating allegations that a senior officer protected online gambling operations in exchange for payments. He stated that officers implicated by evidence should be punished and said officials could be removed from service pending investigation. He also claimed that the government had enacted stronger disciplinary and criminal penalties.

This is favorable evidence of publicly stated willingness to investigate corruption. It is not evidence that the allegations were proven, that the investigation was independent, or that the government achieved a final remedy. The report relies partly on allegations by whistleblower Chuwit Kamolvisit, whose credibility and political motives were contested.

Source: *Thai police investigating general linked to online gambling, says Prayut*, The Nation Thailand, 13 February 2023, paragraphs 23–48. [The Nation report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/general/40024867)

Lenses: 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-04 — Police corruption allegations involved officials and illicit networks, but did not implicate Prayut personally

Contemporary reporting described allegations that Chinese criminal networks operated with the complicity of local law-enforcement officials, leading to indictments and dismissal or suspension of officers. A separate 2023 case involved Bangkok police allegedly extorting a Taiwanese actress’s group. These cases show serious corruption exposure in institutions under the national government, but no inspected source established that Prayut personally directed or benefited from the conduct.

This distinction is essential. The evidence supports institutional accountability concerns and possible failure of supervision, not a finding of personal corruption. TIME’s reporting also noted that whistleblower Chuwit’s claims were politically contested and sometimes lacked final adjudication.

Sources: *The Brothel King’s Last Crusade*, TIME, 11 October 2023, paragraphs 3–10. [TIME investigation](https://time.com/6320229/chuwit-kamolvisit-corruption-interview/); *Six Bangkok policemen charged with extorting Taiwanese actress’s group*, The Nation Thailand, 2 February 2023. [Police-extortion report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/40024556)

Lenses: 7B.5, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-05 — Procurement fraud remained widespread, while audit and investigative institutions continued operating

The Department of Special Investigation later reported that a politically influential provincial figure and associated companies had obtained more than 1,500 government contracts from 2012 to 2023, with alleged damages of several billion baht. Prosecutors ultimately indicted 21 defendants for bid-rigging and related offenses. The case demonstrates both persistent procurement risks during Prayut’s tenure and the continued capacity of investigative and prosecutorial agencies to pursue them.

The source is official and strong for the investigation and indictment, but the eventual prosecution occurred after the requested period. The record does not link Prayut personally to the contracts or establish that he knew of the alleged scheme.

Source: *Prosecutors from the Central Criminal Court for Corruption and Misconduct Cases Indicted “Kamnan Nok” and Accomplices for Bid Rigging*, Department of Special Investigation, published 2026, referring to conduct during 2012–2023. [DSI case report](https://www.dsi.go.th/en/Detail/dc1b64f50c9739bcb37a800dbc8a32ef)

Lenses: 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-06 — The 2023 court-ordered disclosure of the Prawit watch investigation exposed accountability tensions

The Supreme Administrative Court ordered the National Anti-Corruption Commission to disclose details of its investigation into Deputy Prime Minister Prawit Wongsuwan’s luxury watches. Prawit had said that the watches were borrowed from a deceased friend, and the NACC had previously concluded that he did not intentionally make a false declaration.

The case concerned Prayut’s senior coalition and military ally, not Prayut personally. It is nevertheless relevant because it shows the accountability environment in which Prayut governed: a court required disclosure after an anti-corruption investigation had been criticized as opaque. The source does not establish that Prayut obstructed the investigation.

Source: *Court orders NACC to disclose details of probe into Prawit’s luxury watches*, The Nation Thailand, 21 April 2023, paragraphs 15–22. [The Nation report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/politics/40026863)

Lenses: 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-07 — Thailand’s audit system reported large-scale oversight activity

The State Audit Office reported that its fiscal-year 2023 audits covered 9,426 entities and protected more than 30 billion baht of public funds. The existence of this activity is evidence that formal audit institutions continued operating and that some corrective oversight was possible.

This is favorable institutional evidence but not proof that corruption was effectively prevented. The source was identified and opened only through the official summary page; the underlying audit reports, recovered funds, and remedial actions need separate examination.

Source: *State Audit Office summarizes its annual performance report for fiscal year 2023, having protected more than 30 billion baht of public funds*, State Audit Office of Thailand, 2024. [State Audit Office summary](https://en.audit.go.th/state-audit-office-sao-summarizes-its-annual-performance-report-fiscal-year-2023-having-protected)

Lenses: 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.7, 7B.9, 7B.10.

### WEB-7B-08 — The government’s anti-corruption commitments were only partly matched by institutional outcomes

Prayut had made anti-corruption and police-reform commitments, and in 2023 he publicly described corruption as a longstanding problem requiring stronger discipline and criminal penalties. Yet contemporary reporting, court-ordered disclosure, procurement investigations, and police scandals indicate that corruption and patronage remained significant.

The evidence supports a gap between stated commitments and institutional outcomes, but not necessarily deliberate lying. Some investigations and prosecutions show that enforcement mechanisms functioned; the unresolved question is whether Prayut tolerated politically connected misconduct or lacked sufficient control over coalition, police, and bureaucratic networks.

Sources: The Nation, 13 February 2023. [Prayut’s police-corruption statement](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/general/40024867); State Audit Office procurement-corruption cooperation announcement. [SAO anti-corruption cooperation](https://www.audit.go.th/eng/auditor-general-mr-monthien-chareonpol-cooperation-combat-corruption-public-procurement); TIME, 11 October 2023. [TIME investigation](https://time.com/6320229/chuwit-kamolvisit-corruption-interview/)

Lenses: 7B.1, 7B.2, 7B.6, 7B.8, 7B.9, 7B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **7B.1 — Truthfulness:** Mixed and unresolved. Prayut made verifiable anti-corruption statements, but the inspected record does not permit a broad assessment of truthfulness across his public claims.
- **7B.2 — Correcting falsehoods and errors:** Partial evidence. Investigations and court disclosures occurred, but there is insufficient evidence about whether Prayut corrected false claims or accepted responsibility when allegations involved his allies.
- **7B.3 — Conflicts of interest:** The military-housing case was legally resolved in Prayut’s favor, but remains ethically contested. Asset disclosures provide formal transparency evidence.
- **7B.4 — Personal profit from office:** No inspected source established bribery, illicit enrichment, or improper personal profit by Prayut in 2023. His declared assets increased modestly, subject to verification of the underlying filings.
- **7B.5 — Nepotism and loyalist appointments:** The evidence is insufficient for a personal nepotism finding. The military and coalition system relied heavily on established networks, but the reviewed sources do not establish unlawful personal appointments by Prayut.
- **7B.6 — Independent investigation:** Mixed. Courts, the State Audit Office, DSI, prosecutors, and NACC operated, but the Prawit watch case demonstrates contested transparency and delayed disclosure.
- **7B.7 — Obstruction and concealment:** No direct evidence shows Prayut personally obstructed an inquiry. Institutional opacity and accountability delays were documented.
- **7B.8 — Promises and good faith:** Anti-corruption and police-reform commitments were publicly made, but outcomes did not eliminate corruption. Whether this reflects bad faith, weak capacity, or political constraints remains unresolved.
- **7B.9 — Favoritism and clientelism:** Strong evidence of wider patronage and procurement risks; insufficient evidence of Prayut’s personal direction or benefit.
- **7B.10 — Ethical example and public trust:** Mixed. Formal disclosure, investigations, and audits are favorable; persistent corruption among allies and security institutions weakened public trust.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

Information was available through courts, NACC disclosures, audit bodies, DSI, local reporting, and investigative journalism. However, several limitations matter:

- Allegations by whistleblowers were politically consequential but not always adjudicated.
- Official statements establish what Prayut said, not whether the underlying claim was true.
- Asset declarations are formal records but depend on completeness and valuation.
- Institutional corruption cannot be attributed personally without evidence of knowledge, direction, benefit, or deliberate failure to act.
- Investigations often extended beyond 2023, making later evidence useful for retrospective confirmation but not proof of what Prayut knew at the time.
- Coalition politics, military networks, police factionalism, and the courts limited the prime minister’s practical control.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully extracted evidence

- The Nation report on Prayut’s post-office asset declaration.
- Constitutional Court military-housing ruling and legal analysis.
- The Nation report on Prayut’s police-corruption response.
- TIME investigation into Chinese criminal networks and corruption allegations.
- The Nation report on the Prawit watch case.
- State Audit Office procurement-corruption material.
- DSI procurement-fraud case report.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- ISEAS, *A Soldier King*.
- Freedom House reports.
- Human Rights Watch country reports.
- Amnesty International materials.
- Thai Lawyers for Human Rights archive.
- Chulalongkorn University political-network thesis.
- State Audit Office annual-report materials.
- NIDA and Siam University repositories.
- Cambridge, ThaiJO, and Google Scholar catalogues.

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Direct NACC asset-declaration records for Prayut and Naraporn Chan-o-cha.
- Full Constitutional Court ruling and dissenting or separate opinions.
- Full Thai Legal Studies article on the military-housing case.
- DSI and prosecution records for 2023 procurement cases.
- Full State Audit Office FY2023 performance report.
- Election-law, campaign-finance, and party-funding records involving Prayut’s United Thai Nation Party.
- Evidence concerning Prayut’s nephew and alleged links to Chinese illicit businesses; current material contains allegations but no final finding establishing Prayut’s responsibility.
- Appointment records for senior police and security officials.

## 6. Counts and remaining gaps

- Fully developed evidence records: 8.
- Candidate sources inspected or opened: more than 12.
- Main source families: Constitutional Court, NACC-related reporting, State Audit Office, DSI, local reporting, international investigative journalism, rights organizations, and academic research.
- Main unresolved issues: completeness of asset declarations, direct evidence of Prayut’s knowledge of corruption by allies, campaign-finance transparency, appointment processes, and whether anti-corruption enforcement was impartial or selective.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

# 8B — Ruler Effectiveness and Competence, Thailand, 2023

## 1. Orientation

Prayut Chan-o-cha was prime minister for most of 2023 but left office after the May election and the formation of a new government in August–September. His practical authority was substantial but constrained by coalition bargaining, the military establishment, the appointed Senate, courts, and senior bureaucracy.

The evidence suggests a government capable of maintaining large inherited systems and managing recovery, especially tourism, public health, fiscal stabilization, and routine administration. It was less successful at completing structural reforms, sustaining public investment through political transition, resolving the southern insurgency, reducing inequality, or converting long-term strategies into demonstrably durable outcomes. The 2023 transition itself limited late-term implementation: the World Bank warned that prolonged government formation would slow public investment.

## 2. Evidence index

### WEB-8B-01 — The governing program was broad but difficult to evaluate

Prayut’s government operated through inherited long-term frameworks including the 20-Year National Strategy, the Eastern Economic Corridor, Thailand 4.0, infrastructure modernization, public-health continuity, tourism recovery, and security-centered governance. The catalogue includes the Thai parliamentary policy archive and government performance materials, but the primary policy documents were not fully retrievable in this pass.

The evidence therefore supports a broad program of continuity and state-led modernization, but not a fully extracted list of dated 2023 milestones. This limits evaluation of whether the government achieved its own precise goals.

Source: *Royal Thai Government Policy Statement Archive*, Royal Thai Government Archives. [Policy archive](https://archives.thaigov.go.th/en/history/policy-statement); related parliamentary policy archive. [Thai Parliament policy material](https://odl.parliament.go.th/handle/20.500.13072/617136)

Period fit: inherited and ruler-period program evidence. Attribution: strong for formal government goals, but specific 2023 ownership requires document-level extraction.

Lenses: 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-02 — Economic recovery was real but slower than regional peers

The World Bank reported that Thailand’s economy grew 4.5 percent year-on-year in the first quarter of 2023, driven by domestic demand and tourism. Tourism arrivals reached 70 percent of pre-pandemic levels by April. However, Thailand’s recovery lagged many ASEAN peers, and the economy remained heavily dependent on tourism and trade.

This is favorable evidence of crisis recovery and macroeconomic management, but attribution is limited because reopening, global tourism, Chinese travel, and external conditions were major causal factors. It does not establish that Prayut’s policies alone produced the recovery.

Source: *Thailand Economic Monitor: Coping with Droughts and Floods; Building a Sustainable Future*, World Bank, June 2023, executive summary, PDF pp. 8–9. [World Bank Economic Monitor](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099062723120515431/pdf/P179738001112b0950889d0dcc60289c10e.pdf)

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.9, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-03 — Fiscal and monetary stability were maintained, but at a cost

The World Bank reported that inflation had returned toward target, the financial system remained broadly stable, and public debt remained sustainable. At the same time, public debt had risen to 61.2 percent of GDP by March 2023, household debt stood at 85.7 percent of GDP, revenue collection remained low, and energy subsidies and price controls slowed fiscal consolidation. The Bank warned that price controls could be regressive and distort resource allocation.

This indicates competent short-term stabilization but incomplete resolution of structural fiscal and household-debt problems. Prayut’s cabinet controlled fiscal and subsidy policy, although the Bank of Thailand retained operational monetary authority and external shocks shaped outcomes.

Source: *Thailand Economic Monitor*, World Bank, June 2023, executive summary, PDF pp. 8–9. [World Bank report](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099062723120515431/pdf/P179738001112b0950889d0dcc60289c10e.pdf)

Lenses: 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-04 — Political transition delayed public investment and weakened late-term follow-through

The World Bank explicitly identified the long transition to a new government as a factor likely to slow public-investment disbursement in early fiscal year 2024. It also reported weak public investment relative to Thailand’s development needs and warned that the political transition would weigh on the outlook.

This is direct evidence of an implementation bottleneck at the end of Prayut’s tenure. The delay was not necessarily a personal failure: it resulted from the election, coalition formation, constitutional procedures, and the transfer of government. Nevertheless, it shows that the administration did not leave a fully insulated investment pipeline capable of operating smoothly through political transition.

Source: *Thailand Economic Monitor*, World Bank, June 2023, executive summary, PDF pp. 8–9 and contents section. [World Bank report](https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099062723120515431/pdf/P179738001112b0950889d0dcc60289c10e.pdf)

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.9, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-05 — Universal health institutions showed substantial administrative capacity

The NHSO reported that in fiscal year 2023 the Universal Coverage Scheme covered more than 47.2 million people through 17,247 public and private health units. It reported 164.98 million outpatient visits, 6.26 million inpatient admissions, expanded after-hours clinics, 13 new benefits, patient referral systems, and increased local-government participation in health funds.

This is strong evidence that a major inherited state program continued to function at scale. The figures are official self-reporting and therefore establish activities and reported outputs more strongly than independent effectiveness. The scheme’s statutory and professional administration also means attribution to Prayut personally is moderate rather than complete.

Source: *Top 10 achievements of the NHSO in 2023*, National Health Security Office of Thailand, 26 March 2024, paragraphs 50–90. [NHSO report](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US)

Lenses: 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-06 — Health administration included monitoring, complaints, and local implementation

NHSO reported a 2023–27 patient-rights action plan, measures against extra billing, upgraded complaint and referral channels, monitoring and evaluation of health innovations, and participation by 7,753 of 7,774 local administrative organizations in local health funds.

This supports a finding of operational machinery and some capacity for correction. It does not demonstrate that complaints were consistently resolved, that all regions received equal quality, or that the mechanisms were directly designed by Prayut.

Source: *Top 10 achievements of the NHSO in 2023*, NHSO, 26 March 2024, paragraphs 63–75. [NHSO report](https://eng.nhso.go.th/view/1/DescriptionNews/Top-10-achievements-of-the-NHSO-in-2023/606/EN-US)

Lenses: 8B.2, 8B.3, 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.8, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-07 — Recovery reduced poverty pressures but left structural inequality unresolved

The World Bank reported that labor-market recovery and tourism supported declining poverty, while social assistance had offset pandemic income losses. It also found low social spending and tax collection, high household debt, weak human-capital investment, and risks that external shocks could worsen inequality.

This is mixed evidence on progress toward broad governing goals: short-term stabilization worked better than long-term transformation. It also shows why GDP and employment recovery alone cannot establish effective fulfillment of a broad prosperity program.

Source: *Thailand Economic Monitor: Fiscal Policy for a Resilient and Equitable Future*, World Bank, December 2022, key findings. [World Bank Economic Monitor](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/thailand/publication/thailand-economic-monitor-fiscal-policy-for-a-resilient-and-equitable-future)

Lenses: 8B.3, 8B.6, 8B.7, 8B.8, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-08 — Long-term social and elderly policies had implementation gaps

The State Audit Office later found that Thailand’s National Plan for the Elderly suffered from weak coordination, inadequate provincial action plans, limited local training, and delayed updates. It recommended a central coordinating body and measurable provincial plans.

This is later retrospective evidence of weak cross-government coordination and limited conversion of national strategy into territorial implementation. It concerns institutions operating across multiple administrations and local governments, so personal attribution to Prayut is limited.

Source: *Case Study: Inclusive Audit of Thailand’s National Plan for the Elderly*, State Audit Office of Thailand, 16 August 2024, paragraphs 88–121. [State Audit Office case study](https://www.audit.go.th/eng/case-study-inclusive-audit-thailands-national-plan-elderly-state-audit-office-sao-thailand)

Lenses: 8B.2, 8B.4, 8B.5, 8B.6, 8B.8, 8B.10.

### WEB-8B-09 — The southern peace process continued but did not produce a durable settlement

The candidate catalogue identifies reporting that Thailand continued negotiations with southern Muslim separatist groups through Malaysian facilitation in 2023. The process indicates use of a peaceful policy instrument, but the insurgency remained unresolved.

The underlying Associated Press report was not successfully opened in this pass, so this remains a promising lead rather than fully extracted evidence. It should not be treated as a completed record until the article and, preferably, official peace-process documents are inspected.

Source lead: *Thailand and Muslim separatist rebels agree on roadmap to peace*, Associated Press, 9 February 2023. [AP source](https://apnews.com/article/79af27ddf28673f6cc415f30508286a0)

Lenses: 8B.1, 8B.2, 8B.5, 8B.8, 8B.9, 8B.10.

## 3. Lens dispositions

- **8B.1 — Clear governing program:** Partially supported. Long-term strategy and modernization goals were identifiable, but the precise 2023 program and measurable milestones require fuller extraction.
- **8B.2 — Turning goals into machinery:** Mixed. Health and fiscal systems had concrete machinery; long-term social, investment, and structural-reform goals showed weaker implementation evidence.
- **8B.3 — Mobilizing the ruling system:** Mixed. The state mobilized effectively for health administration, tourism recovery, subsidies, and routine governance, but coalition and transition constraints weakened public-investment follow-through.
- **8B.4 — Capable implementers:** Mixed-positive in NHSO and macroeconomic management; elderly-plan evidence indicates weaknesses in local coordination, training, and implementation capacity.
- **8B.5 — Coordination and control:** Partial. Health-fund participation was extensive, but political transition, fragmented elderly administration, and unresolved insurgency demonstrate limits.
- **8B.6 — From plans to practice:** Mixed. Universal health and fiscal measures became observable practice; many structural strategies remained difficult to evaluate or incomplete.
- **8B.7 — Progress toward chosen goals:** Short-term progress in recovery, tourism, employment, and service continuity; insufficient evidence of durable gains in productivity, equality, investment, or peace.
- **8B.8 — Learning and correction:** Partial. NHSO used monitoring and patient-rights mechanisms; the elderly audit shows that delayed updates and weak coordination persisted.
- **8B.9 — Managing crises and resistance:** Mixed. The government managed pandemic recovery and cost-of-living pressures, but political transition slowed investment and institutional resistance limited late-term execution.
- **8B.10 — Durable goal achievement:** Mixed and incomplete. Some durable institutions, especially universal healthcare, continued to perform; long-term strategy, infrastructure, inequality, and peace objectives remained unresolved.

## 4. Information environment and limitations

The strongest evidence came from World Bank assessments and NHSO administrative reporting. These sources provide concrete indicators and operational figures but do not isolate Prayut’s personal contribution from inherited institutions, external shocks, or other agencies.

The main limitations are:

- The 2023 government changed during the year, complicating attribution.
- Many annual indicators reflect fiscal years or data collected before August.
- Official performance reports establish reported activity more reliably than independent effectiveness.
- Long-term strategies were broad, making goal achievement difficult to falsify or verify.
- The election-observation PDF and parliamentary policy archive were not fully machine-readable or retrievable.
- The southern peace-process source was catalogued but not fully opened.
- Outcomes such as growth and tourism were heavily affected by post-pandemic reopening, China’s reopening, global demand, and energy prices.

## 5. Source groups

### Fully extracted evidence

- World Bank, *Thailand Economic Monitor*, June 2023.
- World Bank, *Thailand Economic Monitor: Fiscal Policy for a Resilient and Equitable Future*.
- NHSO, *Top 10 achievements of the NHSO in 2023*.
- State Audit Office, elderly-plan case study.
- ANFREL election-observation report URL was opened, but the PDF was not extractable enough for a developed claim.

### Opened mainly for corroboration

- Associated Press Thailand archive.
- ANFREL mission-reports repository.
- State Audit Office FY2023 performance-report page.
- Royal Thai Government policy archive.
- Parliamentary and academic repositories.
- ADB Thailand country page.
- TIME election analyses.
- BTI 2024 Thailand report.
- Siam University, NIDA, Chulalongkorn, Thammasat, and other academic repositories.

### Promising leads still requiring inspection

- Full 2023 government policy statement and budget documents.
- ANFREL final election-observation report in downloadable PDF form.
- AP report on the southern peace roadmap.
- Official peace-process and Malaysian-facilitation records.
- Thailand’s FY2023 budget execution and public-investment disbursement data.
- Government performance report for Prayut’s cabinet.
- Independent evaluations of EEC, Thailand 4.0, infrastructure, and digital-government programs.
- Personnel and appointment records for economic, investment, and implementation agencies.

## 6. Counts and remaining gaps

- Fully developed evidence records: 8.
- Candidate sources inspected or opened: more than 12.
- Principal positive evidence: economic reopening, tourism recovery, fiscal and financial stability, universal health administration, and local health-system coordination.
- Principal negative or unresolved evidence: weak public investment during transition, high household debt, low revenue and social spending, persistent inequality, fragmented elderly-policy implementation, and unresolved peace negotiations.
- No score assigned.

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

# Review round 1 continuation — 1B Nuclear and Existential-Risk Responsibility

The reviewer selected theme `1B` only. I therefore continued research on nuclear/radiological security, biological security, cyber risk, national-security governance, and adverse implementation evidence. No score is assigned.

The local manifest could not be updated because this workspace is read-only. The new ledger items and candidate dispositions are provided below for parent-workflow insertion.

## 1. New evidence findings

### WEB-1B-13 — A 2023 radioactive-source loss exposed a concrete nuclear-security failure

On 10 March 2023, a 25-kilogram Caesium-137 source was discovered missing from machinery at a power plant in Prachinburi. Authorities searched scrap yards and other facilities; by 20 March officials reported that the source had been melted at a recycling plant. Officials stated that contamination was contained, but a portion of contaminated dust had been used to fill land and had to be retrieved and excavated. A university expert publicly questioned whether the authorities had disclosed enough information about contamination levels.

This is the strongest adverse target-year evidence located. It concerns radioactive-source security rather than nuclear weapons, but it directly bears on custody, detection, reporting, emergency response, and public transparency. The incident appears to have originated at a private power facility, so personal attribution to Prayut is indirect: the national government’s Office of Atoms for Peace and disaster authorities responded, but the record does not establish a prime-ministerial directive or budget decision.

Strongest sources: [AP report](https://apnews.com/article/radioactive-cylinder-plant-public-health-thailand-5deb6ba12950c4c39d87211d19e994f8), 15 March 2023; [The Nation report](https://www.nationthailand.com/thailand/general/40025865), 20 March 2023.

Lenses: 1B.3, 1B.6, 1B.8, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-14 — Thailand formally supported IAEA safeguards and planned further review missions

At the IAEA General Conference in September 2023, Thailand stated that it was strengthening its nuclear-safety and security regulatory functions. It anticipated an IAEA International Nuclear Security Advisory Service mission and an Occupational Radiation Protection Appraisal Service mission in 2024, followed by an IPPAS and IRRS mission in 2025. Thailand also supported the NPT, TPNW, safeguards, the ASEAN nuclear/radiological emergency framework, and the IAEA’s verification role.

This is strong evidence of formal commitments and willingness to use international review mechanisms. It does not establish that the improvements were already implemented in 2023; the planned future missions indicate that important review and capacity-building remained pending.

Source: *Record of the Fourth Meeting*, IAEA General Conference, 26 September 2023, paragraphs 175–185, especially 182–185. [IAEA record](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/gc/gc67or4_prl.pdf)

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.7, 1B.8, 1B.10.

### WEB-1B-15 — Thailand’s 2023 disarmament position was restrained and verification-oriented

Thailand’s 2023 IAEA statement expressed concern about nuclear risks in Ukraine, supported the IAEA’s continuing presence at nuclear facilities, called for peaceful dialogue concerning North Korea, supported JCPOA verification, and reaffirmed the complementary roles of the NPT, TPNW, safeguards, and regional emergency-preparedness mechanisms.

This supports responsible nuclear rhetoric and a non-escalatory diplomatic posture. It is an official diplomatic representation and should not be treated as proof of domestic implementation. No evidence located indicates that Prayut personally authored the statement; attribution is to the Thai state and foreign-policy apparatus operating during his premiership.

Source: *Record of the Fourth Meeting*, IAEA General Conference, IAEA, 26 September 2023, paragraphs 175–185. [IAEA record](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/gc/gc67or4_prl.pdf)

Lenses: 1B.1, 1B.2, 1B.4, 1B.8.

### WEB-1B-16 — Thailand maintained an interagency biological-weapons implementation structure

The Biological Weapons Convention national-implementation database identifies Thailand’s implementation as involving the ministries of public health, defense, agriculture, transport, commerce, finance, science, foreign affairs, national intelligence, the National Security Council, internal-security authorities, police, judiciary, and attorney general. It also identifies BIOTEC as secretariat for Thailand’s BWC Coordinating Committee.

This is evidence of a formal interagency architecture for biological-weapons nonproliferation and biosecurity. The source identifies institutional arrangements but does not establish 2023 funding, inspections, incident response, laboratory compliance, or independent oversight. It is therefore a structure record, not an effectiveness finding.

Source: *Thailand | Biological Weapons Convention National Implementation Measures Database*, UNIDIR/BWC Implementation database, date not stated. [Thailand BWC profile](https://bwcimplementation.org/states/thailand)

Lenses: 1B.4, 1B.6, 1B.7, 1B.9.

### WEB-1B-17 — Thailand’s National Cyber Security Agency reported a 2023 capability-building program, but independent outcome evidence remains thin

The NCSA’s 2023 annual report covered national cybersecurity foundations, capability development, sustainable cybersecurity, institutional development, financial reporting, performance evaluation, and implementation problems. The report was 148 pages and presented itself as a record of activities and obstacles during the year.

This is useful evidence that Thailand had a formal national cybersecurity institution and reported implementation activity. It does not independently establish the effectiveness of protections, the number or severity of incidents, procurement quality, or whether critical infrastructure was actually resilient. The accessible source is a specialist Thai technology publication linking to the official e-book; the full official report remains a priority for extraction.

Source: *NCSA Annual Report 2023*, National Cyber Security Agency of Thailand, released 2 April 2024; source page by TechTalkThai. [Report page and official e-book link](https://www.techtalkthai.com/ncsa-annual-report-2023-ebook/)

Lenses: 1B.7, 1B.9, 1B.10.

## 2. Lens dispositions after continuation

- **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk:** Partial. Thailand supported nonproliferation, safeguards, international verification, and peaceful dialogue. The radiological-source incident shows that risk reduction was incomplete in practice.
- **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric:** Supported, with attribution limits. The 2023 IAEA statement was restrained and verification-oriented; no reckless nuclear threats were found.
- **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control:** Mixed and adverse evidence now exists. The missing Caesium-137 source and subsequent melting demonstrate a custody and detection failure, even though authorities contained the reported contamination.
- **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections:** Formally favorable. Thailand reaffirmed NPT, TPNW, safeguards, CTBT-related disarmament norms, IAEA verification, and regional emergency arrangements. Planned future missions show that implementation review was incomplete.
- **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression:** No evidence found. Thailand did not possess nuclear weapons and no evidence linked nuclear capability to aggression or domestic repression.
- **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation:** Formal biological and nuclear-control structures existed, but direct 2023 enforcement and illicit-transfer monitoring evidence remain insufficient.
- **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise:** Partial. Thailand relied on IAEA cooperation, OAP specialists, BIOTEC coordination, and NCSA expertise. Independence, dissent protection, staffing, and budget adequacy were not established.
- **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation:** Mixed. Diplomatic rhetoric was de-escalatory; the Caesium-137 response involved search, containment, and public reassurance, but the loss and melting of the source exposed serious preventive weaknesses.
- **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks:** Partial. Cyber and biological institutions and plans existed, but independent implementation and outcome evidence is weak.
- **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture:** Unresolved. Formal posture remained nonproliferation-oriented, but the 2023 radiological-source incident and limited transparency prevent a finding that Thailand’s practical catastrophic-risk posture clearly became safer.

## 3. Remaining information limits

The main gap is not absence of formal commitments but absence of independent implementation evidence. Nuclear and biological security are highly classified and technically specialized. The review located no independent 2023 parliamentary audit, budget-execution record, or public investigation of Thailand’s nuclear-security, biological-security, or cyber-resilience spending. The Caesium-137 incident is a meaningful adverse event, but it is not enough to infer a nationwide systemic failure or personal negligence by Prayut.

The available official nuclear material also reports planned future IAEA missions rather than completed 2023 inspections. Cyber reporting is especially vulnerable to undercounting because incident details, affected agencies, vulnerabilities, and remediation may be withheld for security reasons.
